We Need a Mayor for President
2026-09-16

We Need a Mayor for President

TIME | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings A view of the White House on March 11, 2022. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings The 2028 presidential race is already taking shape, and there’s a Zohran Mamdani-shaped hole in it. New York City’s relentlessly upbeat new mayor is filling potholes, removing scaffolding, and, yes, taxing the rich. Not since FDR has there been a happier liberal warrior. But assuming nobody amends the Constitution, the Ugandan-born Mamdani will not be going from Gracie Mansion to the White House. Yet after breaking bread with local leaders across the political spectrum and around the world, from Athens, Greece to Atlanta, Georgia—including with Mayor Mamdani in my hometown of New York—I’m convinced that a mayor should be our next president. Only three mayors have ever become president. Calvin Coolidge, who served as mayor of Northampton, Massachusetts, from 1910 to 1911 before later becoming the 30th U.S. president in 1923, is the last U.S. president with past mayoral experience. Coolidge and before him, Grover Cleveland, are remembered as saving money for their cities, a mission that can be translated to protecting constituents from today’s roaring inflation. I would argue that the fact that we haven’t had a mayor as president since then explains the discontent and lack of community in American politics. Too often, it seems that the higher up you go in the political food chain, the less functional things become. As the federal government fails to address skyrocketing costs, the case for a mayor-president has never been stronger. The things Washington hasn’t been able to tackle or has failed to accomplish for decades are precisely the muscles mayors flex every day. The mayors I’ve met are can-do optimists, free of the cynicism and vitriol that has poisoned much of American politics. Many were political outsiders who felt called to serve. Haris Doukas was a mechanical engineering professor before being elected mayor of Athens, Greece. Patrick Payton, the now-former mayor of Midland, Texas, was a pastor. Each signed up for a relatively unglamorous job that’s part CEO, part building inspector, part mediator—and, on a bad week—part first responder and grief counselor. In my experience, mayors tend to be deeply committed to their fellow human beings and their livelihoods. Henriette Reker was stabbed in the neck the day before being elected mayor of Cologne, Germany, while still in a coma. Her would-be assassin opposed Germany’s “influx of refugees,” and yet when Russia invaded Ukraine in 2022, a fully recovered Reker didn’t hesitate to welcome more than 100,000 Ukrainians fleeing the war. This commitment is also crucially expressed in empowering citizens. A Mayor President can launch citizen assemblies, like the kind Mayor Carlos Moedas has championed in Lisbon, that invite citizens to participate in community decision-making and budgeting that have the same force as legislative action. Plus, mayors are unavoidably accessible and accountable to the people they represent. While some members of Congress likely fear accountability from their constituents, Mayors encounter impromptu town halls whenever they pick up milk at the grocery store. As I witnessed on several occasions, residents were happy to fist-bump or prob their mayor in the middle of our interviews. When not engaging face-to-face with their constituents, mayors from Santiago to Düsseldorf have rolled out apps that allow residents to track garbage collection or report power outages directly to City Hall. In the birthplace of democracy, Mayor Doukas keeps a monitor in his office tracking complaints as they’re received and how fast they’re resolved. It took the FDA roughly two months to respond to the cyclosporiasis outbreak that sickened many. Can anyone imagine a mayor ducking the public that long? Mayors have to prioritize pragmatic solutions over ideologically-driven culture wars. For instance, unlike most in his party, Republican Miami Mayor Francis Suarez doesn’t mock climate change; instead, he invested in bolstering the city’s infrastructure to withstand climate-exacerbated hurricanes. In Chile, Santiago’s cop-turned-Mayor Mario Desbordes has reduced kidnappings without castigating his opponents as soft-on-crime. Desbordes considers Felipe Muñoz Vallejos of Estación Central, a mayor from an opposing party, not just a frequent partner but a friend. “When one holds these positions,” he told me, “one must do so thinking of the citizens, not of one’s personal political standing.” The mayors I have met aren’t afraid to collaborate and adopt successful democratic experiments around the globe—a far cry from the Trump Administration’s focus on dismantling diplomacy, flippantly waging wars, and unilaterally renaming bodies of water. In Toronto, Mayor Olivia Chow levied a vacancy tax on speculators hoarding unused homes that has raised millions to address Toronto’s housing shortage. And Lisbon’s Mayor Moedas modeled his policy of making public transit free for residents under 23 and over 65—more than doubling ridership—on similar efforts in Estonia and Luxembourg. The inspiration for Lisbon’s new system of tunnels and reservoirs, which saves millions of euros by using rainwater for street-cleaning and irrigation, came from London. Finally, mayors aren’t afraid to innovate, especially when it comes to guaranteeing Americans dignified lives. Local leaders understand it’s not freedom if you can’t afford it. That’s why Atlanta Mayor Andre Dickens turned COVID-era shipping containers into housing for city residents—and, blocks away, opened a city-owned grocery store in a downtown food desert. This is not to say that all mayors throughout history have always acted in a manner befitting the Oval Office. In the 1800s, many municipal governments were faceless corrupt bureaucracies. It was said you couldn’t fight City Hall. But as modern mayors have evolved to solve problems for everyday Americans, and the country prepares to choose its next leader, wouldn’t it be nice if a product of City Hall were fighting for all of us? Why AI Makes Customer Service Worse, And What Companies Can Do To Fix It
2026-09-16

Why AI Makes Customer Service Worse, And What Companies Can Do To Fix It

TIME | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings I am on hold with music that is no doubt the same music played on loop in the fifth circle of hell. It’s been 35 minutes. It will be 50 minutes before the system will hang up on me because I am not a leaf in its automated customer-service decision tree. I am angry and frustrated. There is no way to talk to an actual human. My credit card company referred me to a monitoring service when its data systems were breached (again). The monitoring service sends me a sexual-predator alert about my file. What? I can’t get into my account because my password won’t work, and I have to be on the site to change my password. I call the customer-service number and try to get myself routed to a human by saying the word “agent” 25 times. By the time I get to an actual human, she sympathizes and says that the magic word is “fraud”—you always get to a human immediately because in the case of fraud, the credit card company is the victim. The fraud humans can route you to other humans who can help me change my password. It turns out that no one thinks I’m a sexual predator, I just live in the vicinity of one. Sound familiar? In the increasingly automated world of customer service, custom service—actual humans who can solve problems and answer questions—is on the wane. What we increasingly get are automated customer-service systems that are frustrating to deal with and don’t solve our problems. Most people call customer service only when something is not working, but it seems that these systems are designed to address “common” problems—problems that you don’t necessarily have. When there are no people to be found to explain things to, you may end up in an endless loop, having a bot tell you the same annoying irrelevant thing over and over. How is that customer service? In my own fantasy world, customer service is custom service—service that targets my needs and my problems, suggests good solutions, and assists me in implementing them, all via a human who can take into consideration the tangible and intangible contexts relevant to a remediation that fits my needs. Sounds dreamy, doesn’t it?Such solutions actually exist in the real world. For instance, in Northampton there is a store that sells bras. When you go there, there are expert humans who work with you to find and fit bras that work for your body. I understand that this is an experience that half of the people around the world (and many of my readers) will never have, but trust me, it’s wonderful. The experts at the store help triage a myriad of choices of bras: different colors, cup sizes, wired and unwired styles, different fabrics, and other options. They listen carefully as you tell them about the kinds of clothes you wear, your lifestyle, and your preferences. In the end, you can get something that really works for you and enjoy the experience of getting there. That experience is way different than choosing something on Amazon and hoping it will fit. The bra store is all about custom service rather than homogenized, automated customer service. Custom service works there because the bra store is not a mega-company with millions of customers. Plus, we're probably a ways away anyway from automated systems that will find and fit your bra. My experience with automated customer service is that it homogenizes individuals and the problems they are likely to have and separates them from the people who can actually help. Look, I understand that it’s harder and more expensive to do customer service at scale—when you have millions of customers—and that there's no cost-effective way to hire enough people to give everyone a personalized experience. But there’s something else going on, something we should not lose in cyberspace: a real sense of respect for your customer and an understanding that without satisfied customers, businesses can cease to exist. You can do custom service at scale with millions of customers. Some companies do this well and they invariably earn my loyalty. When I have a question about my Fidelity account that I can’t answer by going to the website or chatting with the chatbot, I can call Fidelity. Within a relatively brief amount of time, the bot who answers the phone verifies my voice and connects me with a human who can track down the answer to my question. Something similar happens when I call USAA Insurance. In 2025, USAA was in the Fortune 100 and Fidelity was the third-largest mutual fund company in the U.S. Apple also has a phone number that connects me with real humans who can help solve problems. In addition, they have brick-and-mortar stores with a “Genius Bar” staffed by actual humans who apparently are not allowed to make me feel stupid. (OK, most of these folks are not geniuses, but they understand Apple products and systems way better than I do.) You probably have your own list of companies that make it easy for you to deal with them and easy for you to solve problems. My guess is that all or most of them have knowledgeable humans available early and often. Digital technologies should help us do things better, not worse. Algorithmic efficiency is not a substitute for human empathy and judgment. Can’t we create a cyberspace that has both? Reprinted from Better Tech: Putting People First in Cyberspace by Francine Berman with permission from MIT Press. Copyright 2026. The Best Ways to Stop Yourself From Crying
2026-09-16

The Best Ways to Stop Yourself From Crying

TIME | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings First your eyes start to burn. Then your throat tightens and your face gets hot. And then comes the realization that other people are watching you cry. Tears rarely consult your calendar: They arrive during performance reviews, tough conversations, uncomfortable medical appointments, and other moments when you’d much rather schedule them for later, in private. Fortunately, psychologists say there are ways to interrupt the process—or at least turn down the intensity—without pretending you’re fine. “Even as a therapist, there are definitely times where you’re like, ‘OK, shoot, I need to keep it together,’” says Juliet Kuehnle, a therapist in Charlotte, N.C. She calls that “adaptive suppression.” “It’s intentional, it’s short-term, it’s situational.” Crying isn’t entirely under your conscious control. It involves a complex interplay of brain, bodily, and emotion-regulation processes—which helps explain why a stern internal “absolutely not” doesn’t reliably work. Kuehnle describes crying as “a release valve for your nervous system.” “Crying is sort of your body’s permission to let go,” she says. In one small lab study, women’s heart rates rose just before and as they began to cry, then quickly returned to baseline. Their breathing slowed, too. The findings suggest that crying can begin amid heightened arousal, then become part of the body’s shift back toward calm. Here’s how to tamp down on your tears when they come at an inconvenient time. Don’t wait until the tears are already rolling. Your best chance of postponing them is to act during the early warning period, when your eyes are just starting to sting or your breathing begins to change. “Once someone starts, it’s hard to pull it back,” says Lauren Bylsma, an associate professor of psychiatry and psychology at the University of Pittsburgh who studies crying. “It’s always easier to pull back emotions when they’re in that earlier, more mild stage.” Your emotional starting point matters, too. If you slept terribly, got into a fight with your partner before work, or are already juggling six other stressors, it might not take much to tip you into tears. “If you’re already overwhelmed with everything else going on, then it just might be harder,” Bylsma says. In the heat of the moment, start by slowing your breathing. “My favorite is an extended exhale,” Kuehnle says. You might breathe in for four counts, hold for seven, and exhale for eight—or simply focus on breathing out slowly. “The important thing is that long exhale,” she says. Another option is two quick inhales through your nose followed by one long exhale through your mouth. The goal is to lower your level of physiological arousal before the tears gain momentum. If you’re somewhere you can get away with it—muted on Zoom, for example—Kuehnle also suggests humming, which can have a calming effect. “Those Gregorian chanters were onto something,” she says. If you know you’re an easy crier, practice slow breathing regularly so it’s easier to access when you need it. Tracy King, a clinical psychologist in England, compares it to building strength at the gym. “You have to build stamina, ” she says. The fear of crying can become its own emotional event. You notice tears forming, imagine everyone else noticing, and start panicking about what they’ll think of you. Now you’re upset about being upset, which raises your arousal even more and makes the tears harder to stop. “The key is to stay out of your head and try to remain in your body,” King says. Interrupt the panic by directing your attention toward neutral sensory details. Put both feet on the floor, and notice the pressure of the chair beneath you or the texture of its fabric under your hands. Narrate a few facts to yourself: The carpet is gray. The chair feels cool. I can hear the air conditioner. If possible, take a sip of water. You can also name the emotion to yourself: I’m overwhelmed. I feel powerless. Or simply: I feel bad right now. Identifying what’s happening can make the experience feel less mysterious and help you regain enough composure to decide what you need next. You might remind yourself: I’m having this feeling. I’m going to tend to it after this meeting is over. As Kuehnle puts it, “You’re telling your brain and body, ‘I’m going to get to you.’” Sometimes it’s most helpful to find a convenient reason to pop out of the situation, even briefly. Excuse yourself to the restroom, splash water on your face, and move around for a minute. “Your nervous system can feel very trapped if you’re sitting in a meeting,” King says. “Getting up and going to the loo—just having a bit of movement—can be really helpful.” Other times, holding back tears is a form of self-protection. Kuehnle recently had to put that idea into practice during an unpleasant doctor’s appointment. One thought kept looping through her mind: If I cry, I lose. She felt the familiar urge to apologize, then caught herself. “I was like, ‘Wait, I have nothing to be sorry for—and actually, you’re the one who should be sorry, because your bedside manner is terrible,’” she says. She ended up telling him: “This is all so overwhelming.” She saved the tears for a place where she felt safer. That’s the difference between postponing an emotion and pretending it never happened. Adaptive suppression is “intentional, it’s short-term, it’s situational,” Kuehnle says—and it comes with a plan to deal with what happened later. Sometimes these techniques will fail. If it’s obvious that you’re becoming emotional, acknowledging the moment might be more calming than mounting a cover-up. King once became unexpectedly overwhelmed while speaking at a conference. She focused on someone she knew in the audience and said, “Could you bear with me a minute? I just feel a bit strange.” Simply putting it out there helped. She paused, regained her footing, and continued speaking. “That cuts through that secondary layer of, ‘What are people going to think of me?’” King says. “You’re taking back agency.” A short acknowledgement—“Give me a minute”—puts you back in charge: You’ve named what’s happening and told everyone what you’re going to do next. Resist the urge to apologize. Crying is a human response, not an offense you’ve committed against everyone around you. If you feel compelled to say something after you’ve collected yourself, King suggests replacing “I’m sorry” with “Thank you for bearing with me.” “What we’re trying to do is move out of shame and into some sort of gratitude,” she says. “It’s the shift that we’re trying to make.” You don’t necessarily owe anyone an explanation, either. Crying is “fundamentally an attachment behavior,” Kuehnle says—a “bid for connection or care,” even if that bid is subconscious. If someone is giving you painful feedback, for example, your reaction might already make perfect sense—and speak for itself. Your tears might also tell the other person something they needed to know: Perhaps their tone is unnecessarily harsh, or they need to pause and approach the conversation differently. Of course, not everyone will respond thoughtfully. That can make an already difficult moment feel even worse—but it doesn’t make the feeling wrong. Whatever reaction you receive, King says, remember: “You’re never in the wrong for feeling what you feel.” Delaying tears is one thing; refusing to acknowledge what upset you is another. If you tell yourself you’ll deal with the emotion later, keep that promise. “Sometimes someone says something in a meeting that makes you feel quite small, and then you get on with your day,” King says. “It doesn’t really come to mind again, but your body remembers that.” Returning to the feeling doesn’t necessarily mean crying. Tears aren’t automatically cathartic; whether they leave you feeling better depends partly on the situation and how other people respond. You don’t need to force yourself to cry on a schedule, either. “One person called me and was like, ‘Should I set up a time each day where I force myself to cry?’” Bylsma recalls. “I was like, ‘No, that’s not how it works.’” There’s no universally correct amount of crying, and more isn’t necessarily healthier. “There’s not a normal amount,” Bylsma says. “It’s OK that some people cry more and some people cry less. It doesn’t mean there’s anything wrong with you unless it’s causing a problem for you.” However you choose to revisit what happened, the goal is to give the emotion somewhere to go. “It’s healthy to find a place to express your emotions, whether it’s by yourself or with your friends or your therapist,” Bylsma says. “Crying is just one piece of that.”

DOGE Initiative Resulted in Federal Employees Getting Paid Billions to Not Work, New Government Report Finds

DOGE Initiative Resulted in Federal Employees Getting Paid Billions to Not Work, New Government Report Finds

TIME | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Then-White House Senior Advisor Elon Musk walks to the White House after landing in Marine One on the South Lawn with President Donald Trump in Washington, D.C., on March 9, 2025. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings The federal government spent an estimated $9.5 billion paying employees to not work last year, with most of the cost tied to a Department of Government Efficiency (DOGE) initiative to shrink the federal workforce, according to a new report from the Government Accountability Office. The report, published Tuesday, found that the use of paid administrative leave increased 435% from 2023 to 2025, while associated salary costs rose sixfold. GAO estimated that $6.7 billion—around 70% of the 2025 total—was associated with the Administration’s deferred resignation program. The congressional watchdog analyzed payroll data from 76 agencies representing around 95% of the civilian federal workforce. In January 2025, the Trump Administration offered roughly 2 million federal workers the option to resign while continuing to receive full pay and benefits through Sept. 30. The government-wide offer was meant to encourage voluntary departures under an arrangement known as deferred resignation. The Office of Personnel Management directed agencies to place employees who accepted the offer on paid administrative leave until their departure. Some agencies later conducted additional deferred resignation programs. According to federal workforce data, 139,963 federal employees left the government through deferred resignation programs. The initiative was part of the Trump Administration’s broader campaign under DOGE to cut what it saw as wasteful federal spending. Tech billionaire Elon Musk, who became the public face of DOGE before leaving the Administration in May 2025, initially promised to save the federal government $2 trillion, before lowering the target to $1 trillion. DOGE’s website ultimately claimed $215 billion in estimated savings. That figure has not been independently confirmed. In a separate review of $110 billion in claimed savings, GAO found that some estimates were incorrect or unsupported. (DOGE officially shut down in July, after OPM absorbed most of its functions in November.) Federal data showed a net decline of 271,363 civilian federal employees—around 12% of the federal workforce—between President Donald Trump’s inauguration and July this year. Those reductions were not uniform across agencies. Between December 2024 and January 2026, the largest decline was at USAID, where the workforce fell 95%, according to a separate GAO analysis. Staffing declined 46% at the Education Department, 37% at the General Services Administration, 34% at OPM, 33% at the National Science Foundation, and 31% at the Department of Housing and Urban Development. The Department of Homeland Security, by contrast, reduced its workforce by less than 1%. Some agencies later had to replace workers they paid to leave. The Partnership for Public Service identified 20,557 hires by June 2026 in the same types of roles as employees who left through deferred resignation. OPM Director Scott Kupor defended the program’s economics in a letter responding to the GAO report. Kupor said OPM expects the workforce reductions to save $20 billion per year, while the $6.7 billion was a one-time cost. “This distinction is crucial: The savings recur every year, while the administrative leave costs with the DRP were incurred once,” Kupor said. The Trump Administration has pursued other efforts to reduce the federal government. Trump imposed a government-wide civilian hiring freeze on his first day in office and subsequently directed agencies to hire no more than one person for every four departures, except for positions of national security, immigration enforcement, and other priorities. Agencies were also directed to review whether probationary employees should be retained, prepare plans for large scale layoffs, and consider eliminating positions and functions not required by law. The government has faced extensive litigation over its workforce cuts. In September 2025, a federal judge ruled that OPM had exceeded its authority by directing agencies to fire thousands of probationary workers, although the judge did not order their reinstatement. Separately, the Supreme Court stayed a lower court injunction in July 2025, allowing agencies to move forward with layoffs while litigation continued. Litigation has since shifted to challenges to individual agency layoff plans. Earlier this month, U.S. District Judge Susan Illston ruled that a DHS plan to cut staffing at the Federal Emergency Management Agency (FEMA) by 50% was unlawful. During the October 2025 government shutdown, the Trump Administration took the unprecedented step of threatening permanent cuts, using the funding lapse to accelerate its government downsizing campaign. Federal agencies issued layoff notices to around 4,100 employees and the Administration warned that more than 10,000 jobs could be eliminated. Congress ultimately nullified those layoffs by statute when the shutdown ended in November, and required agencies reinstate employees and provide back pay. On Tuesday, Sen. Patty Murray (D, Wash.), the top Democrat on the Appropriations Committee, accused the Administration of wasting money while weakening public services. “Trump spent billions to push out experienced and badly needed experts across government,” Murray said. “This was the most expensive way imaginable to make government worse.”
The Atlantic
Willem de Kooning Ripped Up the Rules of Drawing
2026-09-16

Willem de Kooning Ripped Up the Rules of Drawing

The Atlantic | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
Most retrospectives unfold like a stately procession. You buy your ticket and proceed through a choreographed sequence of galleries, scrutinizing first the juvenilia, then the promising early work. Next come the triumphal years, and finally (if the artist was lucky) a poignant late phase. An idea of timelessness prevails, enhanced by an enveloping atmosphere of climate control, security, and massive insurance coverage. The lighting is good; the frames are tasteful. Everything looks marvelous. There’s not a whiff of the urgency of the studio, no hint of the emotional or psychological state in which these objects were made. Lost is any suggestion of that feeling, deep inside the creator, of an improvised, inner necessity. “Willem de Kooning Drawing,” at the Art Institute of Chicago, is a classic retrospective—a decorous, deeply researched exhibition about a much-lauded artist at a major museum. In another, more robust sense, however, the show is a giant debauch. It’s frankly (and thrillingly) unseemly. I’ve never been to an art show that takes you so far inside the creative process. You start to wonder if you should even be permitted to get so close. “Art never seems to make me peaceful or pure,” De Kooning once said. “I always seem to be wrapped in the melodrama of vulgarity.” This show, more than any other De Kooning exhibit I’ve seen, delivers on the spirit of that oft-quoted statement. De Kooning, arguably the most widely revered of America’s 20th-century avant-garde artists, took the language of drawing, ripped it up, and rearranged it. Put that way, what he did sounds rebellious. It sounds punk. The exhibition, which will travel to the Rijksmuseum, in Amsterdam, in early October, embraces that spirit. But it has a more conservative purpose, which is to demonstrate that you can’t rip up art’s language effectively—or interestingly—without first really knowing it. De Kooning knew, from long study, how to draw contours. He knew how to create volume using gradations in tone or marks that move in certain ways, how to apply ink or paint with varying degrees of pressure and velocity, and how to employ color relations to suggest depth. Before you can throw all that technique up in the air and have it land again, you need to have the tools. De Kooning was both the best draftsman and the most lavishly gifted painter of the generation of postwar artists (among them Jackson Pollock, Lee Krasner, Mark Rothko, and Helen Frankenthaler) who helped move the art world’s center of gravity from Paris to New York. Born in the Netherlands in 1904, he trained, from the age of 13, as a commercial artist at the Rotterdam Academy of Visual Arts and Technical Sciences while apprenticing with a design firm. The show kicks off with a large drawing, Dish With Jugs, that De Kooning made at the academy. Working in charcoal and conté crayon two days a week for an entire year—600 hours—the young student achieved a remarkable level of photographic verisimilitude in the still life. As one of the exhibit’s curators, Kevin Salatino, writes of De Kooning’s trajectory in an introductory essay for the show’s catalog, the drawing became “the archetype of what he would forcibly reject while retaining the learned skills the work so brilliantly exemplifies, only now putting them to radically different purposes.” That De Kooning could draw like Ingres (or like Picasso during his Ingres-inspired period, from roughly 1917 to 1925) is amply demonstrated by other early works in the show, such as his adoring, fastidiously controlled pencil portrait of Elaine Fried, a fellow artist and his future wife. But, like a libertine desiring undiscovered pleasures, his appetite for new sensations only seemed to grow with time. Drawing has long been regarded as an intellectual activity, something generated, above all, by the mind, with its capacity for conceiving, imagining, and mandating. (You draw to design buildings and invent machines; you “draw up” battle plans and national borders.) “Drawing, as a way of thinking,” Salatino notes of De Kooning, “lay at the core of his practice, the root of everything.” But is it thought, as such, that we’re seeing in De Kooning’s drawings? The critic Peter Schjeldahl, who knew the artist, described him as “a hand with a man attached.” If you can attribute thought to a hand—and I’m willing to admit that you can, but it surely isn’t rational—then this show is indeed a record of thinking. But it may be better described as the record of some more mysterious melting of mind into body. De Kooning came to America as a stowaway in 1926. Residing first in Hoboken, New Jersey, for less than a year before settling in New York City, he remained without legal status for the next 35 years. During an extended period of hard graft as a house-and-sign painter, a window dresser, a show-card letterer, and an advertising-layout man, he encountered various avant-garde artists, including Arshile Gorky, who became a close friend. Under the Armenian American painter’s influence, he gradually shifted his focus from commercial art to modernist fine art, experimenting with different styles derived from Picasso, Miró, and Matisse. Even when ostensibly abstract, De Kooning’s work was populated by bobbling fragments of body parts. He conveyed these with his characteristic wobbling contours and flesh-textured paint. “Content is a glimpse of something, an encounter like a flash,” he once said. “It’s very tiny—very tiny, content.” He proceeded in the studio intuitively, precipitously, as if wanting to catch his subjects unawares. De Kooning had his first solo show in 1948. He broke through to widespread acclaim in 1950 with Excavation, a painting he labored over for many weeks, using a process of patient buildup and repeated erasure. At almost seven feet high and more than eight feet wide, Excavation was the largest canvas he’d ever worked on. (The following year, it was awarded a prize by the Art Institute of Chicago, which subsequently acquired it.) The work was initially inspired by scenes from an Italian movie, Bitter Rice, an art-house hit in Greenwich Village that year, showing young peasant women working in water-soaked fields. But over time, the bodies dissolved into their surroundings and the pictorial space flattened out into a scrim of fleetingly seen forms with hooked and curving contours that push forward from the surface or twist back into shallow space. If, in its genesis, the work recalled Picasso’s proto-cubist breakthrough, Les Demoiselles d’Avignon—the Spaniard’s 1907 painting of five naked women in a brothel—it also pointed the way to what would become a more explicit, and enduring, obsession for De Kooning: depictions of women, wobbly-edged, many of them leering or aggressive, disintegrating before our eyes into a welter of ambient marks. Inspired by prehistoric figurines of such powerful female divinities as the Venus of Willendorf, and by depictions of women by Rubens and Picasso—and riffing, too, on female figures in contemporary cartoons, movies, and magazine advertisements—De Kooning’s women take up a hefty portion of the show. The critic Harold Rosenberg, an early champion of his work, admired the classical balance of Excavation, likening it to “a formula wrung out of testing explosives.” Observing De Kooning drawing, another critic, Thomas Hess, noted that the artist “made a few strokes, then almost instinctively, it seemed to me, turned the pencil around and began to go over the graphite marks with the eraser. Not to rub out the lines but to move them, push them across the paper.” De Kooning’s line, he concluded, “is always under attack.” This ethos of creative destruction remained a guiding principle for De Kooning. The artist’s methods, however, continued to change. His drawings appear to be in a state of constant becoming. Shapes swell and fall away. They coalesce and unravel. De Kooning’s reversals of figure and ground aren’t binary or static, as in classic cubism; they’re in flux, like water moving in water. The show’s emphasis on the muck and mess of the studio may explain why artists in the Chicago area have flocked to the exhibit—and keep on returning, according to Mel Becker Solomon (who organized the show with Salatino, Margaret Holben Ellis, and Charlotte Healy). Solomon recounted to me that she walked through the exhibition with an artist, who told her, “This is what students are missing in their current practice: the tenacity and rigor to keep pushing, experimenting, and searching for what comes next.” What the show demonstrates, in ways that aren’t always obvious with his paintings, is that De Kooning never lost touch with the vocational skills he acquired as a young man. As Rosenberg wrote in 1962, “There was not a trick of the painter’s trade that de Kooning did not have up his sleeve.” Ellis, a paper conservator at the Art Institute of Chicago, lists those “tricks” in the catalog and explains how De Kooning adapted them to purposes for which they were never intended. She describes, for instance, his use of carbon-transfer paper and translucent vellum to copy motifs from one drawing to another. Such processes were typically used to duplicate technical drawings. But De Kooning would apply fragments of the reproductions to new compositions, often in different orientations. He also used frottage, or rubbing—another technique favored by commercial artists—to activate his lines. He would apply charcoal to thin vellum placed over corrugated cardboard or other textured surfaces to give the marks a stuttering look. “Frottage transformed stillness into speed,” Ellis writes of Reclining Nude, a 1963 drawing. This observation approaches the core of De Kooning’s art: More than any other artist I can think of, he gave painted marks and lines different velocities and trajectories. Co-existing in the same composition, those visible shifts in speed, thickness, and direction carved out three-dimensional spaces of ravishing complexity and urgency. Salatino suggests, in his catalog essay, that the artist’s use of frottage “functions, inescapably, as a metaphor for the masturbatory—making and meaning converging in an act dependent upon the probing, rubbing, exploring hand.” Salatino is a first-rate curator, but this sort of writing, to which his introduction is rather too prone, gives art writing a bad name. De Kooning didn’t traffic in metaphors. He wasn’t interested in meanings. There’s a reason Susan Sontag quoted him at the beginning of her essay “Against Interpretation.” Other techniques that De Kooning learned as a commercial artist included cutting out parts of images he found in magazines and inserting them into his compositions, or using masking tape and paper overlays to give his own marks abrupt, crisp boundaries. When drawing, De Kooning loved to use liner and sword brushes, which are favored by sign painters. Both have short handles and extended, bushy bristles. Loaded with ink and applied with control, they allow artists to make long, even lines. De Kooning had mastered their use as a younger man. (Ellis reports that the illustrator Gus Falk, who was taught by De Kooning at Black Mountain College, remembered him applying a perfectly even line to a spinning bicycle wheel by holding the brush steady. “He was in control,” Falk said. “He was interested in exactitude.”) But in his own drawing, De Kooning let rip with liner brushes, swishing them about all over the paper, achieving undulant lines that contract and expand like living creatures. Perhaps the most remarkable of De Kooning’s maladapted techniques was his use of fixatives. The word itself suggests De Kooning’s perversity. Fixatives are sprayed onto vulnerable media, such as charcoal and crayon, to stabilize drawn marks. But instead of spraying a fine, even mist from an appropriate distance, De Kooning aimed the fixative straight at his drawings from close range, creating puddles and rivulets that turned the paper gray, creating strange, smudgy marks and chemical textures. Later in his career, De Kooning tried drawing with his eyes closed or with both hands at once. Long plagued by alcoholism, he was often inebriated. “I’m in my element when I am a little bit out of this world,” he once said. “When I’m falling, I’m doing all right; when I’m slipping, I say, Hey, this is interesting!” The words could sound somewhat pathetic, like a drunk man’s sad, self-soothing rationalization. But the relationship between De Kooning’s virtuosity and the lengths to which he went just to get in his own way is intriguing to consider. Clement Greenberg, the most influential critic of the postwar period, framed the problem in a review of De Kooning’s first solo show. The artist’s “really great facility,” Greenberg argued, risked getting in the way of his ability to express authentic emotion with real originality. “Facility,” Greenberg argued, “has a stubbornness of its own and is loath to abandon easy satisfactions.” For Greenberg, “easy satisfactions” were always close to kitsch. But his assessment was reductive. De Kooning was an immigrant from Europe. His extensive training and virtuosic way with line connected him to an evolving tradition of European drawing that led from Tintoretto, Rubens, Delacroix, and Ingres to, in the modern era, Picasso and Matisse. He would never disown that tradition. But he was in America now. It was the 20th century. Young women were screaming in front of the Beatles. Crowds crammed into theaters to see Marilyn Monroe magnified on the screen. Betty Boop and Minnie Mouse bounced from frame to frame in the cartoons. De Kooning—neither calm nor pure and “wrapped,” as he had put it, “in the melodrama of vulgarity”—longed to incorporate all this impudent American energy into the authority of his European inheritance, and especially into his drawings and paintings of female figures. His efforts to interfere with his own facility were an expression both of his dividedness and of his deep hunger for freedom. Anyone who stands in front of De Kooning’s urgent, sensuously greedy drawings, with their writhing contours, cacophonous colors, and perverse processes, can feel the force of that craving.
The Atlantic
The Real Obstacle to Venezuela’s Recovery
2026-09-16

The Real Obstacle to Venezuela’s Recovery

The Atlantic | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
Days after two deadly earthquakes struck northern Venezuela on June 24, killing at least 6,000 people and injuring tens of thousands more, the Venezuelan Seismological Research Foundation, known as Funvisis, made an unusual public plea: Could whoever had taken one of its GPS sensors please give it back? For 13 years, the high-precision instrument had been bolted to a rocky outcrop in the hills of western Venezuela, quietly tracking the millimeter-scale movements of the Boconó fault, which was the likely source of the first of the two quakes. When Funvisis technicians went to recover the readings the sensor had stored, they found that the machine had gone missing. More than two months after the earthquakes, the organization still does not know who carried it off. The absent sensor is but one gauge of a state that has lost the capacity to govern. Venezuela once operated 250 to 300 seismic-monitoring stations; today, no more than five still function. Many of the engineers, physicians, nurses, firefighters, and rescue workers a country depends on when disaster strikes have long since left Venezuela—part of a generation-long exodus of skilled professionals from state institutions. President Trump has said that the oil-rich country will be run “properly” and “professionally,” but the earthquakes exposed Venezuela’s deficits. The highest expectations and fondest hopes—whether of Wall Street, Houston, or Caracas—will not conjure up the people needed to rebuild a country that has thoroughly hollowed out its professional classes. Read: The vicious cycle Trump may restart in Venezuela In just 20 years, Venezuela went from Latin America’s wealthiest nation to one of its poorest thanks to the mismanagement, corruption, and poor governance that began under the populist president Hugo Chávez, who served from 1999 to 2013, and continued under his successor, Nicolás Maduro. Interim President Delcy Rodríguez, who took over Venezuela after the United States captured Maduro in January, inherited a state that cannot marshall the technical expertise to rebuild what the quakes destroyed or to reconstruct an economy that collapsed long before the ground did. Rebuilding is a matter not merely of money and political will but of human capital. Bureaucrats, planners, regulators, and inspectors turn plans into functioning grids, ports, and clinics. Their absence may be the biggest obstacle facing American policy makers overseeing Venezuela’s reconstruction. Venezuela is not the first country to suffer brain drain to the point of institutional collapse. Afghanistan has reportedly struggled to stand up its health-care sector and civil service ever since many of its professionals fled the return of the Taliban in 2021. Haiti has been losing doctors, judges, and engineers for decades. But Venezuela’s case is one of particularly deliberate self-destruction, in a country where oil wealth helped produce a sizable middle class. Hostility toward the professional class was a hallmark of Chávez’s particular brand of socialism from the beginning. Adherents saw professional skills, when they were not explicitly placed in service of the revolution, as advancing the interests of capitalism and imperialism, the Venezuelan political scientist Guillermo T. Aveledo told me. In 2003, Guillermo García Ponce, a director at a pro-Chávez newspaper, went so far as to describe technocrats at state-owned companies as a “fifth column” to be “wiped out.” “For Chavismo, privilege always comes cloaked in a PowerPoint presentation,” the journalist Francisco Toro wrote in 2007. By dismissing expertise and deliberation as ideologically suspect, Toro added, “Chavismo is left to rely on the will of the leader alone.” The state’s public utilities were among the first institutions to confront the PowerPoint problem. Chávez replaced their professional managers with loyalists who were more compliant than competent, people who used to work in these companies told me. Jacqueline Faría, a Chavista engineer who took over the state-owned Caracas water company Hidrocapital in 1999, invested millions of dollars in a failed program to clean the Guaire River. Now she leads Rodríguez’s reconstruction efforts. In addition to Hidrocapital, the Chávez government proceeded to dismantle the corporate-style leadership of the Caracas metro and Venezuela’s largest electricity company, Edelca, and to place these companies under the control of military officers. It eventually did the same with Funvisis. In 2009, Chávez took direct control of the country’s airports and seaports. Water and power shortages, sometimes nationwide, became commonplace as mismanagement, corruption, and doctrinaire regulations gummed the works of nearly all of Venezuela’s public infrastructure. Consider Edelca’s two dam projects. The company began building the Caruachi dam in 1997, under Chávez’s predecessor, but finished it only after nine long years. It began building the Tocoma dam in 2007 and still has not finished it, despite almost $10 billion in investment. A Brazilian contractor involved in the work later admitted to paying Venezuelan officials and middlemen as much as $98 million in bribes from 2005 to 2016 to secure contracts. A U.S.-Argentine company will now take over the project. Perhaps no sector was more gravely affected by Chávez’s assault on professionalism than the oil industry. And no industry is more crucial to U.S. plans for Venezuela’s recovery—or more central to Washington’s interest in the country in the first place. Before Chávez, Venezuela’s oil company, known by its Spanish acronym PDVSA, was owned by the state but autonomously governed as a commercial entity. It set the gold standard for such an arrangement, expanding into international markets and fully acquiring Citgo, a major Houston-based refinery, in 1991. Much of the Venezuelan state ran on clientelism, but the oil company was meritocratic. Its research arm, the Venezuelan Institute of Oil Technology, or INTEVEP, employed about 170 full-time Ph.D. researchers and held roughly 1,200 American patents in the period before 2003, according to Francisco Monaldi, an energy expert. Jonathan Chait: Trump meant it literally when he said he was taking the oil That was the year Chávez began meddling in PDVSA’s affairs. He fired its board on live television and appointed a military officer to run the company. PDVSA’s workers went on strike in protest. Chávez fired more than 18,000 of them—dismissing, in one stroke, 60 to 80 percent of all provisioning, planning, maintenance, and exploration staff. The finance team was laid off almost entirely; state cronies took over an office that accounted for nearly all of Venezuela’s foreign-exchange earnings. INTEVEP’s scientists were replaced, Pánfilo Masciangioli, a researcher and manager at the institute from 1982 to 2010, told me, “with people who did not even know how important it was to publish a paper.” The institute’s patents fell to about 100 during the decade that followed. “The company’s gray matter was lost,” Monaldi told me. In its place came party loyalists and military men, who filled PDVSA’s vacancies and directed its revenues into Chávez’s populist schemes. Former employees scattered abroad. The number of Venezuelans in Alberta—the epicenter of Canada’s oil industry—rose from 465 in 2001 to 3,860 in 2011, and in Colombia, former PDVSA oil experts helped nearly double national oil production by 2011. Meanwhile, across the border, clientelism swelled PDVSA’s payroll from 28,841 in 2003 to 177,770 in 2014. The effect fully manifested a decade later: Global oil prices fell, and Venezuela’s ruined oil industry collapsed, its output falling from about 3 million barrels a day before Chavez’s PDVSA purge to an average of 512,000 a day in 2020. Employees of Venezuela’s state institutions came under continual pressure. In 2004, more than 2 million Venezuelans signed a referendum to recall Chávez. A lawmaker close to the president drew up what would become known as the “Tascón List” to identify and fire the petitioners from state institutions and companies. Hundreds lost their jobs. At the National Library, “a clique decided who was leaving and who was staying,” Mario Di Giacomo, an information specialist there for nearly 20 years, told me. “Dissent was not acceptable.” Loyalists replaced the professionals, and the library’s collections descended into neglect. Millions of archival records are decaying, Di Giacomo said. The Tascón List was but one way of identifying people for firing. At the Venezuelan Institute for Scientific Investigation, the country’s premier scientific-research center, the physicist Claudio Mendoza made a public comment mocking Venezuela’s nuclear ambitions in 2006 (he noted that the country would achieve little so long as it rejected scientific expertise). He was dismissed four days later. The Chavista zoologist Ángel Viloria took over the institute and systematically purged its scientists from 2006 to 2011. “You were either fired or forced to retire,” Mendoza told me. Venezuela slid from Latin America’s fifth-largest producer of scientific papers in the early 2000s to its 11th by 2022. By 2016, researchers who had emigrated accounted for roughly 33 percent of the country’s scientific publications; at least 68 percent of these scholars held doctoral degrees. Trump has promised that American investors will spend $100 billion to rebuild Venezuela’s “rotting” energy infrastructure, but he hasn’t said who will do the work. The simple truth is that Venezuela has rid itself of the technocrats who could deliver the recovery Washington has promised. Few will return to the bureaucracy that ejected them. Some have emigrated for good; among those who remain, many distrust the authoritarian government or do not wish to be associated with it. Read: The bizarre partnership between the U.S. and Venezuela The United States is currently overseeing talks between the Rodríguez government and the democratic opposition about the country’s recovery and reconciliation. It could use that platform to push for basing appointments to public institutions on relevant expertise rather than political loyalty and, in doing so, help restore confidence among the country’s professionals. Among the hollowed-out agencies that should be high on the list for rebuilding is Funvisis—not only for reasons of public safety but also because the geophysical data that Venezuela no longer collects are the raw material of modern oil exploration. Companies use seismic surveys to image a reservoir before they spend a dollar drilling into it, and Venezuela’s archives are dated, its instruments are scarce, and the geophysicists who once operated both are now working in Calgary or Houston or Bogotá. A state that cannot say whether the fault lines beneath it are moving is poorly placed to tell an investor what lies within the Orinoco Basin. Washington can disburse the oil money and lift the sanctions; it cannot staff the ministries. Only Venezuelans can do that. Ultimately, the country’s recovery will be measured less in barrels lifted or dollars banked than in how many of those professionals will go back to work for the state.
The Atlantic
The Armored Sarcophagus Saving Ukrainian Soldiers
2026-09-16

The Armored Sarcophagus Saving Ukrainian Soldiers

The Atlantic | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
The inside of an armored sarcophagus smells of gunpowder, gasoline, and gangrene. Remotely operated, unmanned ground vehicles, nicknamed MAULs, are revolutionizing casualty evacuations along the most dangerous sections of the Ukrainian front lines. They bristle with drone-jamming antennae and carry bulletproof black pods for carrying wounded soldiers to safety. The MAUL goes in when other options have failed. After every rescue, it is cleaned and disinfected. Vitaly, call sign “Ash,” is a medical-evacuation coordinator with the 1st Separate Medical Battalion. (He asked to be identified only by his first name and call sign for security reasons.) “You scrub and scrub,” he told me, “but the smell—it never goes away.” I asked Ash what MAUL stands for. “It’s not initials,” he explained. “It’s a Star Wars thing—you know, Darth Maul? We’ve got a few nerds here.” The name is in all caps because the battalion’s stencil kit has only capital letters. On the battlefield, these new machines have become priority targets for Russia’s drone pilots. They know that, for the morale of the Ukrainian crews coordinating rescues, losing a comrade in vivid detail on the monitors tracking the mission can be devastating. Russian operators wait until the wounded are loaded, then strike. Aerial drones have created a death zone on the front lines, up to 20 miles wide, where anything caught in the open is likely to be struck. Unmanned ground vehicles began arriving in late 2024, mostly running resupply routes parallel to the front. But a mission in early 2025 proved that they could do more than this—that they were capable of reaching deep into the death zone to remove personnel. That mission began with a Facebook post. In February 2025, near Kupiansk, Ukraine, a Russian advance trapped three Ukrainian soldiers from the 154th Mechanized Brigade behind enemy lines. All were wounded, two severely. For weeks, unit commanders attempted to pull them out. “We tried everything,” Kseniya Vynohradova, a veteran of the 154th who now leads an online support group for the unit, told me. Every effort failed. “The brigade dropped supplies by drone,” Vynohradova told me, “but there was no way to get the guys out.” After a month of fending off Russian attacks and surviving on air-dropped rations, the soldiers were ready to give up. Their wounds had turned gangrenous, and their chance of survival was near zero. “I did not know what else to do,” Vynohradova said, “so I turned to Facebook.” The first person to offer hope was Viktor Dolhopyatov. In a secret facility in central Ukraine, he runs Burevii, one of the country’s most innovative defense-tech companies. Russian bombardments have shattered most of the windows, but this superficial damage, common to thousands of buildings across the region, offers a kind of camouflage. From here, Burevii has transformed the modern battlefield. When Dolhopyatov read Vynohradova’s Facebook post, he answered immediately: “Tell us more, we want to help.” Burevii’s engineers were developing a ground drone at the time. It would need a few modifications for a mission deep into the death zone—anti-drone netting, software updates, and a trailer attachment to carry three soldiers at once—but it could be ready within days. Olha Garbovskaya had joined the company two weeks prior and threw herself into the mission. “We called our machine Ardal,” she told me, after a son of the Greek god of craftsmen and technology. With engineers still attaching its final pieces, Ardal was rushed to the front. The rescue began at dusk, with daylight fading but temperatures warm enough to partially mask Ardal’s heat signature. From a command bunker, Burevii’s engineers watched the mission unfold. High-definition screens displayed a ground’s-eye view from the drone. Overhead feeds tracked Ardal’s route to the stranded soldiers, who followed its progress over their radio. Without warning, a massive heat bloom filled the infrared monitors in the bunker. Ardal had struck a mine. “Our hearts sank,” Garbovskaya said. “The commanders told us we had less than a 1 percent chance of succeeding, and we feared they were right.” Over the radio, the stranded men responded stoically: “We can’t take any more—just finish us off.” They begged to be bombed by Ukrainian drones. The team from Burevii asked them to hold out a little longer. “Give us 48 hours,” Dolhopyatov said. “We have another machine.” The second Ardal followed a different path through the minefield, and overhead bomber drones scoured the route to clear suspected mines. Ardal finally reached the men’s trench. They emerged ragged and wounded, but alive. Overhead, Russian drones closed in. From the command bunker, medics offered encouragement over the radio: “Climb in fast—we’re getting you out.” Ardal carried them to safety, under the watchful eye of Ukrainian aerial drones. In the command bunker, Garbovskaya surveyed the scene. More than 50 people—pilots, medics, electronic warfare crews, engineers—had all assembled to save three men. For most of history, recovering injured personnel from an active battlefield has not been a military priority. If the wounded could not make their way to safety, they were left until the fighting stopped. Serious injuries almost always ended in death, and the ratio of wounded to killed was about 3 to 1. During the First World War, evacuating wounded soldiers became its own professionalized combat discipline. Many injuries were still fatal, but the ratio of wounded to killed began to improve. By the time of the Vietnam War, the widespread adoption of helicopters and the doctrine of the “golden hour”—the narrow window in which medical intervention is most likely to save a life—drove the survival ratio to 5 wounded for every soldier killed. During America’s wars in Iraq and Afghanistan, rapid evacuation and advanced medical interventions brought survivability to historic highs. For every American soldier killed in those wars, approximately 10 survived their injuries. But in Ukraine, the death zone has made rapid evacuation all but impossible. Survivability has fallen, with a wounded-to-killed ratio of about 5 to 1. Units such as the 1st Medical Battalion are trying to change that. The old battlefield math held that in many instances, rescuing a wounded soldier required more resources and risk than could be justified for a single life. But Ukraine has opted for a different way of calculating. Read: Ukraine may be just the ally America needs The military has introduced a points system that assigns varying values to different missions, rewarding units with credits that can be traded for new gear and supplies. Under the “ePoints” scheme, a routine resupply run earns two to three points; a casualty evacuation earns upwards of 70. On today’s battlefield, saving a single Ukrainian soldier is worth more than destroying a Russian tank. Inside the death zone, Ukraine’s trenches are so difficult to reach that medics have had to reimagine the golden hour. Wound treatment must be provided on-site, by fellow soldiers. There simply are not enough medics for every trench. Andrii Fedoryshyn, call sign “Jared,” is a trainer with the 1st Med. “The best thing we can teach a soldier is combat first aid,” he told me. “It’s more important than knowing how to shoot.” The numbers bear this out. Less than 1 percent of the injuries seen by the 1st Med are gunshot wounds, and a soldier is far more likely to be called on to aid a buddy than to engage with the enemy. Everyone near the death zone, even journalists, carries a tourniquet. Many soldiers carry two or more, in the event that shrapnel severs multiple arteries. Once immediate care is provided, the waiting begins. “For a front-line soldier, evacuation can take weeks,” the deputy commander of the 1st Med, who asked to be identified by only his first name, Igor, told me. “We once evac’d a patient who had a tourniquet on for 33 days.” That soldier’s unit tried everything to get him out: six attempts, six failures, and multiple armored vehicles lost in the process. The decision was made to send in a MAUL. “He lost the limb,” Igor said. “But we saved his life.” As battlefield resources, MAULs are severely limited. Still, they are treated as expendable. Soldiers’ lives matter more. On average, two MAULs are lost for every five lives saved. I asked Ash, the battalion’s evacuation coordinator, if he ever got sentimental about the machines. “They’re tools,” he answered. “We don’t get attached.” The platoon commander, call sign “Devi,” interrupted. “Except for this one,” he said, pulling up a video on his phone. It showed a MAUL that had lasted 10 missions, surviving drones and mines and saving the lives of 11 soldiers. After it was finally destroyed, its operators compiled a tribute video and posted it online, endowing the dead MAUL with a small but devoted fan club. Officially, the logo of the 1st Med is a coiled snake, the ancient Greek symbol for medicine. The MAUL drivers have an unofficial logo of their own: a coffin overlaid with a progress indicator. They call themselves the “Tomb Raiders,” because, as Devi explained, “we bring people back from the dead.” Hence the movie posters plastered around the bunker, featuring Angelina Jolie in her Tomb Raider role. Opposite a wall of monitors, a scantily clad Jolie is covered in stickers featuring the coffin-and-timer logo. Each one represents a life saved. “When we fill the whole poster,” Igor explained, “we’ll auction it off to buy more machines.” As much as medics value their MAULs, the soldiers I spoke with hated them. “No windows, no ports,” one told me. “I would rather have less protection and see what’s coming at me.” Once sealed in, a patient’s only link to the outside is a single ray of light streaming through the hinge seam and an intercom connected to the medic. On a range far from the front, I rode inside a new MAUL undergoing testing. The coffin-like pod was stifling, and the bumpy ride nearly shook the bones out of my body. “I understand why soldiers hate it,” I told Devi. He received the feedback like a bad Yelp review. “Fair enough,” he said, “but a soldier able to complain is a soldier who survived.” The overwhelming majority of those rescued by a MAUL live to talk about it. In the command bunker, a casualty call came in: severe internal bleeding, with developing sepsis. A MAUL set off. Overhead surveillance drones tracked its progress as the bombers scanned the route for potential mines. Ash checked in via radio about the soldier’s condition. From the overhead feed, we saw the MAUL stop at a tree line. Two Ukrainian soldiers emerged from the foliage. One helped load the other into the sarcophagus, sealed the lid, and disappeared into the woods. Inside the command bunker, a calm professionalism prevailed. No voices were raised, except for Ash’s; he spoke loudly into his headset so that the patient could hear him over the noise of the machine. Midway through the eight-mile return journey, the electronic-warfare team detected a heavy Russian suicide drone, a Molniya, lurking overhead. The MAUL took cover, let the Molniya pass, then continued to the transfer point where a medical team awaited. As it approached the rendezvous, the Ukrainians spotted another approaching drone. “Three kilometers, closing,” came the call. The patient was quickly lifted into an armored vehicle, which sped off toward the field hospital. Ash’s job was done. He took off his headset and slumped into a couch. In the dim light, I noticed for the first time that he was wearing Crocs. His T-shirt, a Polish streetwear brand, said Ghosh. I sat next to him, somehow exhausted even though I had done nothing but observe. Through the walls I felt the thump of nearby drone strikes. No one else in the bunker seemed to notice them. I asked Ash what he did before the war. “Musical theater,” he said. “I was a performer.” I asked about his greatest fear as a combat medic. “Losing one of our guys en route,” he answered. Not seeing the gore of injuries, or wondering whether his bunker would be hit. His heaviest burden was the thought of a disabled MAUL, one of his patients trapped inside, unable to make it home. In the Russian military, by contrast, being wounded and abandoned is the norm. At a POW camp in western Ukraine, I spoke with several Russian prisoners of war. “I was wounded in the legs,” one prisoner, who asked that I withhold his name for fear of reprisal, told me. “There was no rescue—my own guys left me to die.” The first medical attention he received was from the Ukrainian military. Reliable figures for Russia’s wounded-to-killed ratio are difficult to pin down, but conservative estimates place it at about 2 to 1, worse than the survival ratio of Russian soldiers during World War I. At the foothills of Ukraine’s Carpathian Mountains, far from the front lines, lies a rehab facility for injured soldiers. I met Pavlo there, four months into his recovery from injuries sustained trying to rescue another soldier. He had successfully driven his truck to the pickup point for his wounded comrade when he was struck by a Russian drone. Shrapnel entered his abdomen, tore through his bladder, and broke his pelvis. Someone dragged him into a trench. Pavlo begged to stay there, remembering the wrecked and burning vehicles littering the road he drove in on. Over the radio, the medics insisted, “If you do not leave, you will die for sure.” Read: Ukraine planned to swarm Moscow airports with AI-guided drones At dusk, the unmanned ground vehicle came. Over the next three hours, the teams behind it—medics, ground-drone operators, bomber- and aerial-surveillance-drone pilots, and electronic-warfare crews—performed the intricate choreography needed to carry Pavlo to safety. From the top of a Carpathian ski resort, under a cloud-dappled sky, Pavlo recounted every detail, from his failed attempt to rescue his fellow soldier to the moment he went into surgery. Every few minutes, we glanced skyward to scan for threats. We were safe, but such habits die hard. Pavlo would soon return to his new wife, and he knew that this would have been impossible without the ground drone and the humans who operated it, all working together to save this one soldier. “I never met them,” Pavlo said. “A robot carried me, but people saved me. So many, just for me.” He looked up again. “I don’t even know their names.”
The Atlantic
Cuba’s Old Economic Model Is ‘Exhausted’
2026-09-16

Cuba’s Old Economic Model Is ‘Exhausted’

The Atlantic | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
In September 2010, an ailing Fidel Castro made a striking admission. The Atlantic’s Jeffrey Goldberg had asked him whether the Cuban model was still worth exporting. “The Cuban model doesn’t even work for us anymore,” Castro bluntly told him. Sixteen years later, Carlos Fernández de Cossío, one of Cuba’s top diplomats, offered me a similar assessment of the state of the revolution, nearly seven decades on. In an interview in Washington, D.C., Cossío told me that Cuba’s Soviet-influenced system of central planning and state control “is exhausted.” The world, he said, has changed, and Cuba has to change with it. He stressed that Havana is open to pursuing that transformation in partnership with Washington. But coercive economic pressure, he argued, will make the task only more difficult. Coercive pressure is, in fact, the Trump administration’s strategy. Since President Trump returned to the White House last year, his administration has put an economic squeeze on Havana, including sanctions and fuel-shipment restrictions that have contributed to severe shortages and prolonged blackouts on the island. Trump’s approach to Cuba reflects a mix of ideological opposition, national-security concerns, and economic opportunism. Some within his administration object to Havana’s partnerships with U.S. adversaries, including Russia, China, and Iran. (Cossío claimed that Iran’s presence in Havana consisted of one Persian restaurant.) The administration has also cited Cuba’s failure to cooperate with U.S. law enforcement on migration concerns. This year, the two governments appeared open to working out some of these issues and acknowledged having direct contact. But Cuba’s foreign minister, Bruno Rodríguez Parrilla, told reporters in Havana earlier this month that neither negotiations nor an agenda for future talks has been established, despite Havana’s willingness to stay in touch. Since the U.S. military ousted Venezuelan President Nicolás Maduro in January, Trump has repeatedly warned that Cuba could be next. In May, CIA Director John Ratcliffe traveled to Havana to deliver Trump’s message that Washington was prepared to engage on economic and security issues, but only if Cuba made “fundamental changes.” Days later, the head of U.S. Southern Command met with Cuban military officials near Guantánamo Bay. A White House official, in a written response to my questions, conveyed Trump’s view that Cuba “has been horribly run for years” and said the United States is “ready to open a new chapter in the relationship.” The official added: “The only thing standing in the way of a better future are those who control Cuba for their own gain at the expense of the Cuban people.” Cossío and other top Cuban officials have sought to demonstrate that Cuba can change for the better on its own. But the gap between what Havana considers meaningful reform and what the Trump administration says needs to happen remains wide. Washington has demanded substantial changes, and although Havana is open to economic modernization, it insists that its political system is nonnegotiable. Prolonged blackouts have disrupted everyday life, and deteriorating water and sanitation systems have contributed to a surge in illnesses. Hospitals face fuel shortages and electricity disruptions, which affect refrigeration for medicines and vaccines. In eastern Cuba, UNICEF reported earlier this year, 481,000 people were relying on water trucks, and some families were being forced to use unsafe water because fuel shortages constrained pumping and other essential services. Against that backdrop, Cuba has been pursuing its own economic reforms. In June, the government approved 176 measures that expand private business, foreign investment, and private participation in banking, while giving investors a greater role in state-owned companies. The government also began allowing Cubans living abroad to own or invest in private businesses on the island. Subsequent measures have eased bureaucratic restrictions on private businesses and opened additional space for foreign investment, including in tourism. As Cossío and I discussed relations between Washington and Havana—which Cossío was careful to call “bilateral contacts,” not negotiations—he laid out what economic change in Cuba might look like. His country, he said, needs to move toward an economy “more dependent on financial instruments, on the market economy, with a much greater role for the private sector,” while leaving the state as a participant. He described the transformation now under way as the “most significant social and economic transformations” in Cuba in the past 60 years. The U.S. is now applying extraordinary pressure on Cuba, to the point that Cuban officials have accused Washington of seeking to force political change. “They speak openly, openly about military aggression, and they are subjecting the people of Cuba to an unrelenting war,” Cossío told me. The deputy foreign minister made no demands for a new diplomatic initiative or a major U.S. aid package to alleviate Cuba’s distress. Trump, he said, has the power to put the relationship on the right path by removing restrictions on Americans traveling to the island. He could also restore Americans’ ability “to do business in Cuba, to trade with Cuba, to invest in Cuba if they wish.” “We’re not asking for a grant,” Cossío said. “We’re not asking for gifts or favors. We want the U.S. to stop punishing the Cuban economy” and its commercial relationships with countries around the world. This year, for example, Mexico’s state-owned oil company, Pemex, paused its oil exports to Cuba after the U.S. seized Maduro. And the Trump administration backed a lawsuit brought by a private U.S. docks owner against major cruise operators for using docks in Havana confiscated by Cuba’s government in 1960. The cruise companies maintain that their travel operations to Cuba were legal. Read: The schism within the Trump administration on Mexico The Trump administration says that its goal is not simply to open Cuba’s economy but also to pressure the Cuban government into making broader political change. Its policy calls for more freedom, democracy, human rights, and free enterprise in Cuba, while maintaining the U.S. economic embargo and restrictions on transactions that benefit the Cuban military and security apparatus. Secretary of State Marco Rubio, the first person of Cuban descent to hold the position, has repeatedly portrayed the Cuban government as a threat to U.S. national security and argued that its ruling elite maintains control over the economy. This month, as Cuba was rolling out further measures to expand private investment, Rubio announced another round of sanctions against Cuban state-linked financial, mining, and energy entities, saying that the measures were aimed at those who sustain the regime. Cossío stressed that American companies could have a place in Cuba amid the changes if Washington eased its restrictions. “The U.S. could take advantage of that, become an important business partner in Cuba,” Cossío said. “They could invest. They could have a better relation with the emerging private sector in Cuba. They could benefit as a business opportunity.” Mindful that Trump is always seeking another win in relations with other nations, Cossío put it in terms the American president is likely to understand. “They could even claim a victory,” he said. It’s hard to imagine Castro putting the proposition quite that way. Goldberg’s interview with Fidel Castro came as Fidel’s brother Raúl was beginning to loosen the state’s grip on the economy, allowing more small businesses to operate and opening limited avenues for foreign investment. Back then, Cuba was beginning to adopt some of the economic ideas that Washington had long demanded—even as U.S. policy prevented Americans from participating in the experiment. The new measures are a significant loosening of the economic system built around state control. But Cuba remains a one-party state. That may not sit well with the Trump administration, which says Cuba’s economic model cannot fundamentally change without altering its system of governance. Cuba’s critics in the U.S. point to the regime’s record of detaining political prisoners. The government this year announced it was releasing 2,010 prisoners, but Human Rights Watch, citing local advocacy groups, said in April that 700 or so political prisoners remained behind bars. Hundreds more faced house arrest and other restrictions. “The only thing worse than a Communist is an incompetent Communist. And so their system of government has to change because they will never be able to develop economically without those changes,” Rubio said in March. “Economic change is important. Giving people economic and political freedom is important, but they come hand in hand. They come together.” Cossío said that Cuba had been considering a transformation since at least 2011, but had hoped to undertake it under less punishing circumstances. After the opening made by the Obama administration, when the two countries restored diplomatic relations and the U.S. eased restrictions on travel and some commerce, Havana believed it might finally be able to move forward. The subsequent rollback of most President Obama–era policies in Trump’s first term, followed by more limited engagement under President Biden, changed that calculation. Now, Cossío said, Cuba has concluded that it cannot wait. “The country has changed. We’ve changed demographically. Our human resources have changed. The world has changed,” he told me. “We’ve come to the conclusion this year that, in spite of that, we cannot delay any more the transformation that we need to put in place in Cuba.” Sixteen years after Castro acknowledged that the Cuban model no longer worked, Havana is openly trying to evolve—and Cossío is telling Washington that it can either try to force the transformation from the outside or participate from within. But U.S. administrations, past and present, have long been skeptical that a system that has resisted changes for decades can meaningfully transform without radical changes at the top.
