The Rules for This Election Keep Changing
2026-09-16

The Rules for This Election Keep Changing

The Atlantic | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
Bethany Mann has put thousands of miles on her 2014 Toyota RAV4 while campaigning for office in Missouri’s Third Congressional District. The problem for Mann, her opponents, and the hundreds of thousands of voters who live there is that for months, no one has known precisely where the district’s boundaries are. Missouri jumped last year into the nation’s redistricting wars, pushing through a new congressional map backed by President Trump that would secure an additional Republican House seat in November. The plan has mostly collapsed, leading to a year of legal fights and confusion for voters. The partisan battle reached the Supreme Court last week and spurred a chaotic back-and-forth among the courts that changed the boundaries multiple times within a single hour. For now, the old map, which was adopted in 2022, is back in effect, but Republican candidates are still battling. A hearing in a federal appeals court is set for tomorrow—one day before the state is required to start making absentee ballots available to overseas voters. The mayhem in Missouri is the latest example of how uncertain and volatile the midterm elections have become. Republicans started the redistricting battles last year to gain partisan advantage during the midterms, and Democrats quickly followed. Missouri and nine other states have since redrawn their maps. On Monday, the Court blocked a Trump executive order that would have dramatically restricted voting by mail, a decision that Trump called a “big loss for Republicans, and America itself.” Voting-rights groups celebrated the decision, which they say prevents chaos. But with fewer than two months until Election Day, election-related litigation is still winding its way through the courts, and Trump continues to claim that the electoral system is rigged. “We’re talking about last-minute changes to election infrastructure, which could lead to either confusion or affect the outcome of the election,” Nate Persily, a Stanford University law professor, told me. “And it is a serious problem, particularly in our current context when voter confidence in the election infrastructure has collapsed.” Missouri’s saga began shortly after the state legislature passed the new, GOP-friendly map at the urging of the White House last September. Opponents seized on a quirk in the state constitution that empowers citizens to petition for a referendum on an act of the state legislature. In December, an advocacy group submitted more than 305,000 signatures asking for a vote before the new map would be put into effect—which would leave the old map in use for the election this fall. Secretary of State Denny Hoskins, a Republican, had until August 4, the state’s primary date, to certify the referendum for November’s ballot. For months, it’s been unclear which map would actually be used. Democratic candidates, including Mann, told me that they’d stumped through both the new and old versions of their district—a choice that took extra manpower, organizing, and money. Others, including Representative Bob Onder, a Republican whom Mann is challenging, told me that they’d campaigned off of the revised 2025 map. Quinta Jurecic: Rules designed to create chaos “We knew that they were going to be enacting this gerrymandered scheme,” Mann said. “So our strategy, not just in the Third Congressional but as a Democratic Party as a whole, was to beat these congresspeople on any map that they offer.” But the morning of the primary came, and Hoskins had still not certified the referendum. He directed election administrators to hold the primary under the 2025 map. At 4 p.m. that day, an hour before the referendum deadline, Hoskins rejected the petition on state constitutional grounds. Candidates won their primaries based on the gerrymandered map. A month later, the situation got even more complex. On September 3, the Missouri Supreme Court unanimously ruled against Hoskins, finding that the referendum petition was legal and should not have been rejected. It ordered the 2022 map to be used in November. Hoskins appealed the decision to the U.S. Supreme Court, which rejected his arguments last Tuesday. Thirty minutes later, a federal district judge ruling on a separate lawsuit ordered that the 2025 map must be used in November. Citing that decision, Hoskins announced that Missouri would use the new gerrymandered map—leading the Missouri Supreme Court to threaten him with contempt of court. The U.S. Supreme Court quickly chimed in again and restored the old map, for now; Hoskins was briefly held in contempt for continuing to insist that the 2025 map was valid, but he quickly switched his position to match that of the U.S. Supreme Court. Legal experts told me that it is highly unlikely that the appeals court would change the map again, but as the past two weeks have shown, nothing is certain. Both sides agree that the situation is a mess. Republicans I spoke with blamed the Missouri Supreme Court for rejecting the 2025 map after it had already been used for a primary. Democrats—and the majority of legal experts I spoke with—blamed Hoskins for waiting until the last available hour to reject the petition, a move that effectively forced the primary to be conducted with the 2025 map, which his party drew up. They said that the arguments should have played out in court months ago. Hoskins disagreed. “I take my job and oath of office very seriously,” Hoskins told me. “I met that deadline, my constitutional deadline, of August 4. I would say those that don’t like that deadline and want to see that deadline change, they need to contact their lawmaker.” Some of the federal officials whose seats are on the line are, like voters, trying to figure out what’s going on and who is to blame. Onder, who is suing to reinstate the new map, told me that he and voters “operated on the assumption” that the 2025 map was the correct one. “To the extent this has been thrown into confusion and chaos, the fault is entirely [on] the part of the Missouri Supreme Court.” Representative Emanuel Cleaver, whose seat was supposed to be wiped out by the new map, told me that he had been campaigning “all over the state” over the past few weeks because of his uncertainty about which district he’d be in. “There’s been a fog over the state of Missouri,” Cleaver said. “We are now spending time trying to erase the confusion.” Read: The election system wasn’t built for this The bulk of the work is now in the hands of local election administrators, who are scrambling to prepare for the midterms on a condensed timeframe and with a confused electorate. Kurt Bahr, a Republican and the St. Charles County elections director, told me that he’s printing ballots under the old 2022 map but is preparing contingency plans in case courts once again change which map to use. In Boone County, where about 40 percent of voters would shift into a new district under the 2025 map, the clerk Brianna Lennon has provided a lookup tool so voters can see their districts under both maps. She said that she and her staff have been working to inform voters about what’s happening without causing needless panic. “These things are always being argued over and fought over by people who don’t have to run the election,” Eric Fey, the Democratic director of elections in St. Louis County, told me. Election administrators, he said, are the “folks that are left holding the bag at the end of the day.” Last year, Onder posted a clip of a speech in which he recounted speaking with Trump about redistricting. “I said, ‘Mr. President, do you want us to draw you an extra congressional seat in Missouri?,’” Onder said, adding that the state, which voted for Trump by 18 points and has Republican supermajorities in the state legislature, was poised under the old map to send “two progressive Democrats” to Congress. Missouri’s 2025 map aims to net an additional Republican seat in the House by carving Kansas City—long represented by Cleaver—into three districts that extend into rural central Missouri and strongly favor Republicans. The fate of Independence Boulevard Christian Church, in northeast Kansas City, exemplifies this gerrymandering tactic, known as “cracking.” Under the 2025 map, the church is in one district, the parking lot in another, and a market across the street in a third. “Before, there already were people who were saying [voting] doesn’t matter; they had already felt disenfranchised,” Mindy Fugarino, the pastor of the church, told me. “I think there are some folks just using this as another reason to disengage.” The “downward spiral” of extreme partisan gerrymandering over the past year risks disillusioning people, Richard Pildes, a constitutional-law professor at New York University, told me. “It leads to many voters feeling the system is being manipulated, that the system’s rigged,” he said. Read: Out of the gerrymandering darkness, a new hope for reform Both Democrats and Republicans in Missouri told me they feel cheated as the map continues to change. St. Charles County Republican Central Committee Chair Bob Eno told me he believes that the state supreme-court ruling took away people’s chances to choose their own representation by shifting the state back to the old map after primary votes had already been cast. Pat Harcourt, who lives in Wright City, initially thought that her Democratic ballot was incorrect for the August primary. But it wasn’t—her address had moved into a different district under the gerrymandered map. “And here I am writing postcards to people, ‘Your vote counts,’” Harcourt told me. “Well, I don’t know. Maybe it counts, but it may not be in your congressional district.” Mitchell Clem, a Republican voter whose district also moved under the new map, told me that he doesn’t like gerrymandering but understands that Missouri is part of a national trend. “We need to fix this,” Clem, who lives in the St. Louis suburb of O’Fallon, said. “It’s just gotten out of hand.” The drawing of congressional districts has always seemed subjective, and the rules for drawing them have been scrutinized since gerrymandering was first deployed as a political tactic more than 200 years ago. But as partisanship continues to deepen, the rules keep shifting far too late in the process, and voters try, perhaps in vain, just to keep up.
The Atlantic
The American Age Is Over
2026-09-16

The American Age Is Over

The Atlantic | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
The American age that followed World War II was bound to end eventually. What’s shocking is how suddenly that moment has arrived. In the past six months, America’s power—long exemplified by the world’s best warfighting equipment and most highly trained military personnel, a global network of bases and logistics systems, and military and diplomatic partnerships with all corners of the world—has proved insufficient to defeat Iran, a repressive, economically stagnant regime armed with cheap missiles and drones. For years, virtually everyone thought that, in a crisis, Washington could draw on deeper reserves of money, troops, munitions, and diplomatic support than any other nation. Even when U.S. forces faced setbacks in conflicts such as Vietnam, Iraq, and Afghanistan, a widespread assumption about America’s fundamental strength reassured allies and deterred foes. Yet that assumption also promoted a complacency that has left the U.S. military exposed now that even inexpensive, mass-produced weaponry can be guided with deadly accuracy. President Trump did not erode U.S. capabilities on his own. But Trump’s war with Iran has revealed just how the underlying dynamics of global power have shifted—and just how much American might has decayed. Since Britain’s postwar retreat from the Middle East, the United States has been the most important military power in that volatile region. In 1980, the Carter administration asserted that America would guarantee the free flow of oil and other commodities out of the Persian Gulf to the wider world. The success of the Carter doctrine required hostile governments, which then included the Soviet Union as well as the new theocracy in Iran, to believe that the United States could and would take decisive military action against anyone who dared to close the Strait of Hormuz, through which much of the world’s petroleum, and other vital commodities, has long passed. Until recently, the array of American bases surrounding Iran gave that country’s leaders every reason to fear being overwhelmed militarily if they moved to control the strait. Yet the regime remains intact, even after attacks by the United States and Israel succeeded in killing top Iranian leaders, and its long-range missile and drone attacks managed to elude American air defenses and incapacitated many U.S. facilities. In March, fewer than two weeks into the fighting, The New York Times identified 17 American bases, air-defense installations, and diplomatic facilities that had been hit. Among them were some of the U.S. military’s important staging points: the Fifth Fleet’s headquarters in Bahrain as well as the Ali Al Salem Air Base, Camp Arifjan, and Camp Buehring in Kuwait. The effect of these losses on U.S. operations was nearly instantaneous. A key reason the Trump administration did not militarily escalate with Iran over the summer, despite all of its threats to do so, was the loss of the base in Bahrain—which the Navy had relied on for decades to resupply ships in the Middle East. Instead, the so-called logistical tail for U.S. naval operations had to stretch all the way to Diego Garcia, a British-controlled island in the Indian Ocean more than 2,000 miles from the Strait of Hormuz. Complications to resupply efforts raise costs, reduce operational efficiency, and add to the strain on military personnel. Disrupted logistics contributed to the long deployment of the aircraft carrier USS Abraham Lincoln, which led in turn to low morale. According to Stars and Stripes, families of crew members on the Lincoln feared that the many months at sea were causing serious mental-health problems. For the moment—and likely for longer—the bases taken out of commission by Iranian attacks have been largely abandoned as operational facilities because of the continuing threat to U.S. personnel and equipment. At a recent town-hall meeting, Navy Times has reported, a service member asked Admiral Daryl Caudle about retrieving personal items from the Bahrain base. “We’re not getting back in there anytime soon,” Caudle responded. “I just want to be completely honest with that.” The vulnerability of U.S facilities near Iran is so great and the prospective cost of repair is so large that, as The Washington Post reported last month, administration officials might not want to rebuild them at all. One way to spin the situation is as a “once-in-a-generation chance for the Pentagon to reconsider its presence” in the Persian Gulf region, as the Post put it. But the basic fact is that defending key facilities against even a less technologically advanced foe such as Iran is not something that the U.S. military can currently do. The diminution of U.S. strength has a cascading effect. To maintain its military effort against Iran, the United States has had to strip the western Pacific of vital equipment. The Trump administration finally relieved the Abraham Lincoln last month; taking its place in the Arabian Sea was USS George Washington, which is normally based in Japan. Its normal task is to discourage military action by China—a deterrence that is now greatly weakened. This desperate move only underscores that American military resources, however enormous relative to most other countries’, are not infinite. The war with Iran has depleted stockpiles of some of the Pentagon’s most important and advanced weaponry. By the end of April, cautious studies were estimating that the United States had already run dangerously low in THAAD (Terminal High Altitude Area Defense) munitions, Patriot interceptors, and Tomahawk cruise missiles. Shortages have worsened since then. For years, the United States has focused on building so-called exquisite systems—advanced weaponry that is produced in limited quantities at a high per-unit cost. This approach was in part an adaptation to the post-9/11 War on Terror, in which U.S. forces focused on counterinsurgency and fought enemies that lacked the capacity to mount and sustain serious attacks on American logistics. Under those circumstances, the Pentagon built up a broad array of munitions but not deep stockpiles of them. At current production rates, the United States will need several years to build supplies back to where they were when Trump went to war in February—and even those levels in hindsight look far too weak to maintain America’s long-standing global position amid a shift toward mass-produced precision-guided drones. The erosion of American manufacturing superiority is an old story, but it is a key element for understanding the end of the American age. The U.S. could once outbuild other powers by tapping into civilian production. During World War II, Americans retooled the automotive industry to build aircraft. Today, relative to the rest of the world, the United States does almost no civilian shipbuilding. China, in comparison, makes about 50 percent of the ships in the world and controls about 80 percent of the world’s drone manufacture. Having built its military to prevail in a burst of high-intensity fighting, the United States simply lacks the equipment, stockpiles, and rested forces to engage China for any extended period of time. In the past, the United States could rely on allies to amplify its own power. Nations that shared values or at least interests with Washington have hosted bases, provided diplomatic support for American initiatives, shared intelligence, and sent troops and equipment to support American-led military operations. Today, traditional allies have capabilities that the U.S. sorely needs. For example, Japan and South Korea are the second- and third-largest shipbuilding powers in the world. Rather than recognizing that it needs allies more than ever, the current U.S administration perceives governments that have followed American leadership for decades as free riders, annoyances, even hindrances. Recently, President Trump has been critical of South Korea and praised his supposedly great relationship with North Korea’s dictator, Kim Jong Un. Never mind that South Korea and North Korea are still technically in a state of war, and North Korea has nuclear weapons that could devastate its neighbor to the south and potentially other U.S. allies farther afield. Meanwhile in Europe, the U.S. government has distanced itself from long-standing democracies and moved closer to the Russian dictatorship of Vladimir Putin. Since returning to the White House, Trump has scaled back the American provision of security to Europe. Earlier this year, the Pentagon announced plans to withdraw 5,000 troops from Germany, and the administration has launched a defense-posture review that heralds further cuts. The U.S. is making no secret of its goal of turning over more and more of the responsibility of defending Europe to the Europeans. Leaders in Europe are taking these moves seriously and are making plans to rely less on the United States. Germany is considering whether to help the U.K. modernize its nuclear weapons so that Europe has a nuclear deterrent if the United States pulls out. The United States has even begun to alienate Canada. Trump has regularly talked about his desire to incorporate Canada as the 51st state. He has also launched a trade war that, by his own account, will be “devastating” to the Canadian economy. Americans have now twice elected a president who overtly seeks to reframe traditional alliances as zero-sum relationships in which the United States needs to do less and its former partners, more. Leaders and voters in those countries might reasonably conclude that Americans do not know or do not care what they are giving up. Americans have long benefited from peaceful integration with Canada. The U.S. could go to war with Iran in part because of its large base network in Europe. The disruption of long-standing alliances will be devastating to America’s global position. Other countries are already adjusting to newly revealed American weaknesses. Pakistan, Turkey, and Saudi Arabia—all long-term U.S. partners—recently signed their own defense pact. Now that Iran is exercising control over the Strait of Hormuz, Oman, on the waterway’s opposite shore, is inching toward closer cooperation with Tehran. Other Gulf States are reaching out to Iran as well. Even Saudi Arabia, which for decades based its security on massive purchases of U.S. weapons and the hosting of U.S. forces, must reassess its choices after Trump told its de facto ruler that the U.S. will not assist the country in its war with the Houthis. Meanwhile, South Korea is improving relations with China, and European states are thinking more and more of a European-wide defense association. These are all strong signs that the countries around the world are losing confidence in America’s capabilities—and its intent. Unfortunately for the United States, the administration seems more intent on hiding problems than correcting them. The Defense Department has forced out key staffers at Stars and Stripes and demanded, in an investigation of leaks about munitions shortages and other matters, that certain Pentagon employees take polygraph tests. Even if the United States tries to revert to more of its earlier way of doing business, recent events suggest that its global reach will decrease. How the rest of the world will adapt to that change is hard to say. The American age may not give way to dominance by China, because that country has problems of its own and other states in Asia, such as India, are growing to balance it. Democratic nations that look after their own defense more diligently, rather than relying on American protection, might conceivably benefit. The world might also experience a very chaotic period of wars and competitions. All of history’s previous superpowers have eventually declined. The emergence of East Asia as the center of the world’s economic output was always going to reduce American influence. The transition from one global age to the next is seldom evident in the moment. When Russia invaded Ukraine four and a half years ago, the potential of mass-produced drones had not yet become clear. As recently as February, few experts would have predicted that the United States—a global colossus for more than 80 years—would end up so hamstrung by Iran. But the conflict reveals something important: The American age has come to an end. U.S. global dominance cannot continue without the power sources that brought it about.

John Roberts’ New Role Model Demonstrates Everything Wrong With the Supreme Court
2026-09-16

John Roberts’ New Role Model Demonstrates Everything Wrong With the Supreme Court

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a weekly newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. After the Supreme Court refused on Monday to let him disrupt mail-in voting, President Donald Trump denounced his own appointees and said the court’s recent rulings are so “shockingly bad” that “it won’t be easily possible for our Country to recover.” But if anything threatens the country’s recovery, it is the court’s other rulings, the ones that place the president above enacted law. The court has insulated the president from criminal laws designed to prevent corruption and election interference. It has let him disregard votes by Congress that prohibit him from starting illegal wars abroad. And it has said he can fire the watchdogs Congress installed to check him—replacing independent agency leaders with bootlickers. Yet because of decisions like the mail-ballot ruling, the court is often cast as the last line of defense between the presidency and autocracy. Such a view misunderstands what the court is up to in those rare instances when it has stood up to the president. In each of those cases, what we have witnessed is the court enforcing federal statutes. A judicial duty explicit in the Constitution, the enforcement of federal statutes is foundational to a nation where every individual, including the president, is subject to the rule of law. But alongside this important judicial role, the court has claimed a second, and more troubling function: a power to defy enacted federal law. Since the 1850s, the court has claimed that it may dissolve any statute Congress enacts whenever five justices dislike Congress’ constitutional judgment. The result has been an imperial presidency—along with a court that claims the exclusive power to police or condone the president’s excesses. To save our democracy from this unsustainable situation, the next Congress must replace judicial supremacy with a court accountable to federal law. A court required to enforce federal statutes would still strike down executive actions that violate the Constitution—indeed, a federal statute instructs judges to do precisely that. What the court would lose is a judicial power to immunize the president from the laws Congress has written to restrain him. After the Civil War, Congress began building a multiracial democracy in the United States. It passed civil rights acts that guaranteed birthright citizenship and enacted Reconstruction Acts that secured black suffrage in the South. Congress also faced a white-supremacist president, Andrew Johnson, determined to break its laws. Johnson commanded the military officials responsible for carrying out Reconstruction and he intended to sabotage it. So Congress bound him with legislation. The Command of the Army Act required presidential orders to flow through Gen. Ulysses S. Grant, whom Congress trusted, and it barred the president from removing Grant without the Senate’s approval. The Tenure of Office Act gave the same protection to the civilian officials in charge of Reconstruction. When Johnson appeared to violate that second law by firing Secretary of War Edwin Stanton, the House impeached him. During the impeachment trial in the Senate, Johnson’s lawyers made a novel and controversial claim. Even if Johnson had defied a federal statute, they argued, it was merely to allow the Supreme Court to decide its constitutionality. Members of Congress were horrified. They conceded that a president could object to the constitutionality of a bill by vetoing it during the lawmaking process—but they objected that the president could not wield a second veto by ignoring the law after it was enacted. A Constitution designed to free the people from monarchy would not have handed to the president this “more than kingly prerogative.” Johnson was spared by a single vote in the Senate. But he was also chastened. A president bent on defying federal law was restrained—not by nine justices, but by federal statutes and the representatives who enforced them. Reconstruction’s opponents drew a different lesson. They insisted that the president needed a court powerful enough to protect him from “the meshes” of Congress. As the nation abandoned Reconstruction, a generation of politicians and historians recast Johnson as a martyr to congressional tyranny. Their remedy was to articulate and entrench a new doctrine: that the Constitution makes the court supreme over Congress. They encouraged the court to void any statute that hems in presidential power. It took 50 years for that doctrine to triumph. It arrived carrying a grudge. In 1926, Chief Justice William Howard Taft wrote that studying “the injustice that the radical Republicans did to Andrew Johnson” left him “humiliated as a Republican.” He then used a case before him, Myers v. United States, to condemn the Tenure of Office Act and strike down a Reconstruction-era regulation of the presidency. Admirers cheered that the court had at last vindicated Andrew Johnson. Today’s court has resurrected that project. Just this term in Trump v. Slaughter, the court invoked Myers to shred century-old statutory protections that kept public servants from being replaced with loyalists. Afterward, on Substack, Chief Justice John Roberts wrote admiringly that Taft’s leadership not only protected the president from Congress, but also helped to fend off congressional efforts to rein in the court. Slaughter follows a dangerous pattern of opinions placing the president beyond the reach of enacted law. In Trump v. United States, the court exempted the president from most federal criminal law. The statutes that prohibit corruption, obstruction, and election interference now bind everyone in the country except its most powerful officer. With a subtle procedural order issued earlier this month, the court permitted the president to finish a $400 million ballroom on the rubble of the East Wing he demolished, a project two lower courts held he had no authority to build without Congress. Thanks to the court’s decisions kneecapping the Voting Rights Act and campaign-finance laws, the court has also unleashed dark money on our elections and prized partisan entrenchment over competitive elections and minority representation. Worse yet, the court has made clear it will not tolerate statutory responses to presidential overreach and corruption that any future majority might attempt. If we want a president who can be prosecuted for his crimes and who cannot purge the civil service or send masked agents into our neighborhoods, then Congress must be able to bind the presidency with laws that stick. Sticky laws require limiting the court’s power to dissolve them. Congress cannot use statutes to restrain a president whom the court exempts from duly enacted federal law. Some may object that the court has lately made itself the indispensable man. It struck down the president’s illegal tariffs. On Monday it refused to let the Postal Service impose new rules that would have kept millions of mail-in ballots from reaching voters. It appears poised to continue preserving birthright citizenship from the president’s attacks. Lower courts have blocked unlawful National Guard deployments and lawless Immigration and Customs Enforcement raids. Curb the judiciary now, the worry goes, and you disarm the last sentry on the wall. But look at which power the court is using in each case. Every one of these rulings restraining the president is enforcing a federal statute: the tariff decision enforced the law Congress wrote governing emergency powers; the mail-ballot order enforced the statute governing the Postal Service, which nowhere authorizes it to regulate elections; the birthright citizenship cases enforced a statutory guarantee Congress first enacted in 1866, even before the 14th Amendment; relief against ICE enforced the Administrative Procedure Act, a federal statute that instructs courts to vacate unconstitutional executive actions. By contrast, when the court has defied or rejected a federal statute because it doesn’t comport with how five justices see the Constitution, the court has loosened vital checks on presidential power—sometimes checks that many Congresses, and even many presidents, believed were both constitutional and crucial for a healthy democracy. These two types of judicial power are not two sides of one coin. They are in conflict. A court that defies federal law enables presidents to do the same. The choice we face today is not between a strong court and a weak one. It is between a court that puts the president above the law and a court that holds him to it. But to choose a president beholden to the rule of law, we need to hold the justices to enacted law as well. That choice is ours to make. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. We Found the Most Galling Argument of the Upcoming Supreme Court Term
2026-09-16

We Found the Most Galling Argument of the Upcoming Supreme Court Term

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a weekly newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. Today, more than 100 million Americans live in places where weapons like the AR-15 are banned or heavily restricted. Generations of ordinary citizens fought for these assault-weapon prohibitions in the wake of tragic killings on a mass scale. The gun-rights groups opposing these laws often had more intensity, more money, and more influence in legislative chambers. From the first statewide ban in 1989, motivated by a California school shooting that left five children dead, to Virginia’s passage of a ban earlier this year, the legislative victories were hard-won. Yet the laws in the 11 states that have these bans are suddenly on shaky ground. This fall, the Supreme Court will entertain a Second Amendment challenge to the prohibition of assault weapons in Viramontes v. Cook County. Briefing before the court is in full swing. The challengers—a few individuals who want to own such weapons, along with some gun-rights advocacy organizations—recently filed their brief with the justices, arguing that the Constitution prohibits the people of these states from protecting themselves through law. The challenge directly concerns bans in Connecticut and Cook County, Illinois, but the court’s ruling will apply nationwide. The legal side of the constitutional debate over assault weapons can become consumed with technical details, like muzzle velocity, rate of fire, and the amount of kinetic energy a bullet fired from these weapons transfers to a human body. Or with arcane historical trivia, like the magazine capacity of the air rifle Lewis and Clark took on their westward expedition in the early 1800s. There’s no doubt that measuring mechanical functionality and discerning historical fidelity have become critical components of the conversation. Yet, if left abstract, they can obscure the real human people by whom and for whom these laws were enacted. This abstraction turned to subterfuge in the challengers’ briefing before the Supreme Court last month. The most galling part of their argument is the recurrent invocation of—and self-righteous cloaking of their argument in—the language of democracy. “The decision whether a type of arm is too dangerous for ordinary Americans to possess,” they wrote, “is one that the people are entitled to make, not one that courts are empowered to make for them.” They say this, to be clear, in a brief asking the judges of the Supreme Court to make the decision about what weapons can be possessed over the decision of voters. Because the lower courts upheld these laws, the challengers want the justices to tell the citizens of Connecticut, Chicago, and other jurisdictions like California and New York that they cannot make fundamental judgments about public peace and safety. Later in the brief, the challengers again assert that “whether the danger posed by a particular type of firearm outweighs its utility for lawful purposes is a decision for the American people to make, and judges are not authorized to second-guess that decision.” Yet their precise argument depends upon judges second-guessing—indeed, overruling—the decision that millions of those American people made about these weapons. It seems that judges are empowered to second-guess the decision of the American people if, and only if, gun-rights proponents don’t like it. After all, the American people voted for these laws in more than a third of the country. Connecticut citizens saw an AR-15-style weapon slaughter innocent 6-year-olds and acted to expand their law. The challengers, on the other hand, would have the Supreme Court foreclose that pathway of democratic choice. Contemporary Americans have grown accustomed to viewing the Supreme Court as the ultimate check on majority rule. And, in some areas, it might appropriately act that way. But—to the extent one credits the founders’ views—there’s good reason to doubt they thought nine lawyers in robes should have the unbounded authority to overrule a democratically elected legislature’s choice about how best to balance public good and individual liberty. It distorts the concept of democracy to cloak oneself in the rhetoric of protecting decisions by “the people” while urging an insulated cadre of educated attorneys to tell the parents who buried their children and then devoted their lives to the prevention of gun violence to shut up and sit down; your voice and your vote do not matter here, the challengers demand the court to decree. It would be a tragedy for the court to follow that path in the Viramontes case. It is an approach that is both historically uninformed and normatively unattractive. It squelches the most important right the founders fought to preserve to their posterity: the right to rule ourselves. Fortunately, federal judges have, by and large, rejected these calls to restrict the people’s right to make gun laws. No challenge to an assault-weapon ban prevailed among the federal courts of appeals until after the Supreme Court agreed to hear Viramontes. Conservative federal Judge J. Harvie Wilkinson expressed the matter best when he wrote for a majority of judges upholding Maryland’s assault-weapon ban: “We shudder to imagine the hubris with which a court would disable representative government at the very moment that lethal technologies are proceeding at an accelerated and indeed unprecedented pace.” As we await its decision, one can only hope there are enough Supreme Court Justices still willing to don the humility required to let the people remain their own rulers. Despite the Supreme Court’s Ruling, Trump’s Still Trying to Tip the Midterms
2026-09-15

Despite the Supreme Court’s Ruling, Trump’s Still Trying to Tip the Midterms

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-15

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. On Monday, the Supreme Court blocked Donald Trump’s effort to throw mail balloting into chaos ahead of the November midterm elections. But that was just one prong of Trump’s attempts to tilt races toward Republicans. Today we’ll explain the others. This Supreme Court ruling seems like a big deal. What exactly happened? You aren’t wrong. As the legal expert Richard Hasen explains in Slate, Trump’s executive order would have created onerous new requirements for mail-in voting that risked disenfranchising millions of elderly, military, and other voters. Seven of nine justices apparently saw that potential catastrophe coming and rejected it, prompting a Tuesday-morning tantrum from the president. Trump has repeatedly tried to get the court to take his side on mail-in ballots, but this ruling looks like the end of the line—at least for this year’s elections. OK, bullet dodged. But what else is Trump doing that we should worry about? Trump’s order was backed by his unfounded claims that mail voting is fraudulent, part of his broader obsession with the false notion that Democrats are “cheating” to win elections. Trump has said he wants to “take over” elections, which are currently administered by state-level officials. That decentralized system is actually a bulwark against the kind of cheating Trump claims to care about—and against a sore-loser president trying to change election rules to protect his own interests. Still, Trump’s administration is trying to get states to hand over voter data and hunting through the rolls for alleged fraud. It’s true that individuals have sometimes illegally cast ballots. But many of those cases have been accidents, like when a software error in New Jersey improperly registered noncitizens in 2023 and 2024, and widespread fraud is a myth. Per the Heritage Foundation, a conservative think tank that helped write Project 2025, there have been about 100 cases of noncitizen voting over the past 25 years. Nevertheless, the administration has asked almost every state to turn over databases that include voters’ personal information. Most have resisted, sparking court battles that the administration has overwhelmingly lost. The Department of Homeland Security, meanwhile, has assigned hundreds of employees to parse public voting databases for noncitizens. Some have allegedly posed as voters, potentially violating state privacy laws. Yet Secretary Markwayne Mullin has publicly touted unverified numbers of alleged noncitizen voters and threatened election officials with prison time. But surely those efforts can be contested in court? Democrats and voting-rights groups have already sued over some of Trump’s moves (hence the case rejecting his mail ballot order). But some fear the administration is looking for ways to circumvent courts. Late last month, a whistleblower alleged that the U.S. Postal Service had continued to build an online portal that could’ve thrown out mail ballots en masse despite an order blocking work on it. (The Postal Service claims it hasn’t defied any court orders.) Courts can’t stop Trump from sowing doubt about the soundness of American elections. He has ordered investigations into debunked voting irregularities in states he lost in 2020 and continues to attack voting machines despite there being no evidence that they’ve ever changed election results. That could lay the groundwork to claim there was fraud if certain races don’t go Republicans’ way this fall. And even if judges toss those claims, they may still depress faith in the results. One recent survey found that less than half of Americans expect the midterms to be free and fair, with most Republicans doubting that mailed ballots are counted accurately and most Democrats fearful that Trump will deploy immigration agents to polling places. So, while the voting could ultimately go off without a hitch, the crisis of confidence continues. The midterms are still several weeks away, so let’s turn to a more urgent question: How to enjoy the rest of your evening. My colleagues have some ideas that don’t require an appeal to the Supreme Court. Take a sip: Martinis are savored for their elegant simplicity and refined romanticism. Now their iconic imagery has been repurposed as chintzy merch and wine mom–style decor. Courtney Iseman asks, “When did we as a society collectively decide, We martini, therefore we are?” Reconsider your “smart” home: Cameras are becoming more and more common in home appliances, including in smart ovens, refrigerators, doorbells, and toothbrushes—even as people decry surveillance measures in public. What does this trend mean for our consumption? Steffi Cao has a prediction. Give your brain a workout: What’s an 11-letter “Enya hit with the repeated lyric ‘Sail away, sail away, sail away’ ”? What’s a nine-letter “cutesy term for spooky snakes”? Sharpen your wordplay skills with today’s crossword. Celebrate with Slate: In honor of our 30th birthday, Slate has revived our original 1996 homepage and filled it with some of our favorite stories. Revisit articles by Slate alums Daniel Engber, David Plotz, Emily Yoffe, and more. We’ll be updating this page over the next month, so keep an eye out for more pieces from the vault! Slate was one of the first news outlets I read regularly, so I’m really enjoying the birthday throwback. Happy trails back to 1996, and hope you have a great night! News and Politics - Slate Magazine
Trumpworld Has Found Its New Jeffrey Epstein
2026-09-15

Trumpworld Has Found Its New Jeffrey Epstein

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-15

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a weekly newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. The specter of Jeffrey Epstein continues to haunt the Trump administration seven years after the sexual predator’s death. But the makings of the next Epstein-style scandal—one that ultimately could be even more damaging for Trump and MAGAworld—appears to be unfolding right now. The Tate brothers, Andrew and Tristan, are alleged to be sex predators on a level approaching Epstein and with perhaps even more political clout with Donald Trump. For those who are blessedly unfamiliar with the Tates, they are highly popular “manosphere” influencers who have garnered an international following, particularly among young men, for their particular brand of content focused on their prideful misogyny and flashing of their apparent wealth. They are also accused of running a decadelong international sex-trafficking ring, with credible allegations against them in multiple countries from dozens of accusers involving gruesome acts of sexual violence, abuse, and exploitation against women and minors. And they have many connections to the world’s top political elite, including Donald Trump’s family members and close advisers. Sound familiar? Just like Epstein, the Tates have seemingly leveraged these connections to receive favorable treatment and avoid the full extent of justice. After years of investigations and attempted prosecutions, the Tates find themselves in the crosshairs yet again. In July, they were charged in Britain on rape charges that also included allegations of possession of child sexual abuse imagery—and are currently sitting in a jail in Miami, fighting extradition. And earlier this month, Romanian prosecutors indicted Andrew for trafficking minors, money laundering, sex with a minor, and witness tampering, and Tristan with complicity. But, in a few important ways, the involvement of Trump’s administration and allies with the Tates could be even more egregious than the Epstein controversy. While the Epstein scandal has centered on holding bad actors accountable for past actions and current cover-ups, the Tate brothers’ scandal is a live issue, and there is reason to believe that if the Trump administration buckles in their favor, the brothers could go back to their criminal activities. Unlike Epstein, who generally lurked in the shadows of elite circles, the Tates brazenly and publicly broadcast their depraved behavior for years. The Tates established their popularity by building online and porn businesses, and becoming internet sensations through their aggressive and controversial misogynistic opinions. Indicia of their alleged criminality have been publicly available for years, including Andrew attacking a woman on camera in 2016. In the process, they lured in young women and allegedly sexually abused and raped them. The victims allegedly include minors. Accusations and law enforcement investigations of rape and strangulation have been swirling around the Tates since at least 2015, when they relocated from the U.K. to Romania to escape possible arrest. Rape allegations persisted in the subsequent years, as the Tates continued to grow their online following. In 2023, the Tates and several co-conspirators were formally charged by Romanian authorities, with allegations from about 35 alleged victims, including minors. The Tate brothers were barred from leaving Romania while the prosecution continued, having been labeled flight risks and public dangers. (The Tate brothers deny all allegations of criminal conduct.) Despite this, their following grew. And, just as Epstein used his wealth and social status to develop political relationships, the Tate brothers’ rise in popularity has come with an increase in political influence, which they have eagerly flaunted. The connections between the Tates and Trumpworld multiplied over time, even as accusations against them piled up. They developed a relationship with the president’s youngest son, Barron, who encouraged his father to engage with the manosphere podcasts and ecosystem. They were personally in touch with Donald Trump Jr., who livestreamed with Andrew in 2024 and called the Tates’ Romanian detention in 2023 “absolute insanity.” (The White House has not responded to questions about Barron’s relationship with the Tates.) After the 2024 election, shows of support from top Trump advisers became even louder. Vice President–elect J.D. Vance followed the Tates on X; right before Trump’s inauguration, incoming counselor to the president Alina Habba said she was a “big fan” of the Tates, compared their legal issues to Trump’s, and told Andrew, “If you’re in the States, I got your back.” After Trump took office, one of the Tates’ former lawyers, Paul Ingrassia, became the White House’s liaison to the Justice Department and then the Department of Homeland Security. These connections appeared to pay off almost immediately after Trump’s inauguration. In February 2025, Richard Grenell, the Trump administration’s special envoy, attended the Munich Security Conference with Vice President Vance and seemed to push for the Tates’ release. Vance’s speech to the conference, in part, echoed right-wing critiques of a recent Romanian judicial decision. Meanwhile, Grenell, after being lobbied by the Tates’ PR team to help the brothers, held a side meeting with Romanian officials at the conference in which he reportedly asked how “the Tate boys” were doing. The New Yorker reported that it seemed clear to the Romanian foreign minister that the Americans were supporting the Tates because the Tates backed Trump in 2024, and that the Tates were “playing a role within the bilateral relations” between the two countries. (The White House denied this, and Grenell denied helping the Tates, but has publicly stated: “I support the Tate brothers.”) Nevertheless, Grenell’s and Vance’s behavior appears to have been interpreted as subtle but clear pressure from the Trump administration to release the Tates. Which is precisely what happened days later. Romania lifted the travel restrictions, in the face of fierce opposition by prosecutors, and the brothers gallivanted to meet their MAGA friends in the United States. When they arrived in Miami, Trump’s oldest living political svengali Roger Stone posted a picture with them and wrote: “Richard Grenell secured the release of the Tates.” According to the New Yorker, Andrew “denied that Donald Trump had personally intervened, but said he had learned from powerful people in Bucharest that the restrictions were removed because of American pressure.” With the Tates now finding themselves in law enforcement’s dragnet yet again, the parallels with Epstein are striking. Although the Department of Justice arrested the Tates in July 2026, assisting the U.K.’s extradition efforts under the new criminal charges, and the White House said they wouldn’t get involved in the matter, the brothers are continuing to try to use their influence to pressure the Trump administration. Following the arrest, the Tates’ lawyer said: “Andrew Tate was a material participant in ensuring President Trump’s victory. And if Trump doesn’t support him, and if the next people who are going to be running for office potentially are just like, yeah, down the river he goes, that young cohort of people will break with the Republican Party in the next election.” As the Epstein scandal has taught us, the fact that Trump’s DOJ arrested the Tates does not lessen the severity of what has already transpired, or the risks going forward. The last time the Tates wriggled away from custody and came to the U.S., a woman in California accused Andrew of choking her and battering her head and face; after their encounter, she was diagnosed with post-concussion syndrome. L.A. prosecutors did not ultimately bring charges but she is pursuing a lawsuit against him, including as evidence her medical report and texts in which he threatened to beat her. The same dynamic transpired with Epstein two decades ago: He, too, was arrested and charged but continued to put significant pressure on the government and ultimately received a sweetheart deal. He then went back to his life of luxury and sex crimes. We’ve already seen the Trump administration take aggressive steps to help supporters who have been charged with crimes and convicted, so his administration’s possible involvement in the Tate brothers’ story isn’t necessarily over. The Tates are continuing to challenge their extradition, and ultimately, once they exhaust their legal options, whether they are extradited to face their charges or not would ultimately be up to the Trump administration. The scope of Epstein’s criminal behavior, and the full extent of his social network, was largely ignored while he was at large. Today, the Tates and the apparent depths of their interlocking connections to people around Trump have broadly fallen under the radar of mainstream media coverage. But for younger Americans they are totemic figures in this political and cultural moment, and their influence is particularly pernicious. That’s why there must be full transparency and congressional investigations to unravel what has already happened and expose the full extent of their relationship with Trumpworld, and also to uncover what, if any, efforts are continuing to be made now by their allies to pressure the Trump administration as the Tates continue to face charges in the United Kingdom and Romania. As a member of the legal team representing the victims of Jeffrey Epstein in litigation, I can attest that the struggle to achieve justice after his death has been righteous but hard. The Tates must be held accountable for their actions and neither favor nor fame can be allowed to shield them. The Epstein scandal and what happened to his victims is heinous; the Trump administration had begun to repeat that miscarriage of justice. Hopefully, they won’t be allowed to continue. SpaceX aims Starship for orbit on September 22
2026-09-16

SpaceX aims Starship for orbit on September 22

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
Flight test 14 angling for 10 hours in space and a splashdown in the Pacific SpaceX is aiming for a September 22 launch of its monstrous Starship rocket on a 10-hour mission to deliver 26 Starlink V3 satellites into orbit. The flight, set to launch at 1215 UTC if all goes to plan and the authorities give the green light, will be the first time Starship has gone into orbit, and the mission is planned to fly at an altitude of approximately 275 km. The mission aims for six orbits, followed by a deorbit burn using a single Raptor engine while. The engineers are then planning a splashdown in the Pacific Ocean west of Chile. The Super Heavy Booster will be ditched in the Gulf of Mexico, hopefully under better control this time. On flight 13, the booster made a "hard splashdown," as SpaceX delicately put it, after only eight of the planned 13 engines reignited. The booster used all 33 engines for its boostback burn, but according to SpaceX, "the three center engines showed signs of ice clogging which triggered an early end to the maneuver." In true iterative style, SpaceX tweaked the hardware to improve filtering and updated the software to "enhance relight reliability." SpaceX has also changed Starship's heatshield, including adding curved tiles to reduce heating in tile gaps and "additional retention mechanisms added to tiles in areas deemed to be at highest risk of falling off during ascent." It is also reusing a pair of tiles recovered from the previous flight test. If Starship is to become truly reusable, these tiles will need to fly over and over again. Getting to orbit is by no means guaranteed. The SpaceX team will only command a final burn to enter orbit if there is sufficient hardware redundancy to perform a deorbit burn when the time comes. After all, nobody wants something the size of Starship making an uncontrolled reentry after its orbit eventually decays. Starship has had an eventful test program. Some missions were spectacularly successful, and several boosters returned to be caught by arms on the launch tower. Others were impressive failures. The vehicle is critical to SpaceX's future; it can loft far greater payloads than the company's workhorse Falcon 9 and is an essential part of NASA's Artemis program. For Artemis III, planned for 2027, SpaceX is expected to launch a Starship equipped with the docking apparatus planned for the lunar variant. Reaching orbit is therefore a critical step. ® Flight test 14 angling for 10 hours in space and a splashdown in the Pacific Iranian strikes overwhelmed regional redundancy in Bahrain and left one UAE Availability Zone inaccessible PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity Data protection chiefs call for 'immediate review' of data protection models Downloading a model is increasingly easy. AWS says wartime damage means some Middle East cloud resources are gone for good
2026-09-16

AWS says wartime damage means some Middle East cloud resources are gone for good

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
Iranian strikes overwhelmed regional redundancy in Bahrain and left one UAE Availability Zone inaccessible Amazon Web Services (AWS) says it is unable to restore access to resources and data in some of its Availability Zones in the Middle East after datacenters were damaged during the US war with Iran. In an update to its AWS Health Dashboard, the cloud giant confirmed that anything hosted exclusively in its Bahrain Region (me-south-1) remains inaccessible. "The damage to our infrastructure spanned multiple Availability Zones and exceeded what our regional and multi-AZ services are designed to withstand," the update says. "After a thorough assessment, we have determined that we are unable to restore access to the resources and data hosted exclusively in this Region." After the first Bahrain Availability Zone was damaged in March, AWS advised customers to migrate their workloads to other Regions. The company said most did so before further attacks disrupted a second Availability Zone in April and rendered the entire Region unavailable. AWS has reached a similar conclusion about one Availability Zone in the United Arab Emirates (UAE), saying it cannot re-establish access to the resources and data hosted there. The affected Zone, mec1-az2, is one of three in AWS's UAE Region. Two AWS facilities in the country were hit by drones back in March as Iran retaliated against US-Israeli strikes on its territory. AWS says it continues to work on recovering resources in the Region, including some hosted in the other affected Availability Zones (mec1-az1 and mec1-az3). It adds that "most customers" have been able to restart operations in other AWS Regions by restoring from backups or copying any data that remained accessible. The company says its support teams remain available to help customers move their applications to alternate Regions. The Register asked AWS to comment, but the company declined to add to the information on its Health Dashboard. In AWS terminology, a Region is a geographic area containing multiple isolated Availability Zones. Each Zone consists of one or more discrete datacenters with independent power, cooling, and network connectivity. AWS recommends distributing applications and resources across multiple Availability Zones to withstand the loss of a single location. That approach cannot protect against a Region-wide failure, however. Workloads that must survive such an event require a disaster recovery plan involving another Region. AWS and several other tech companies advised customers after the first strikes to recover whatever resources they could and replicate them in alternate Regions, ideally in Europe, rather than wait for services in the Middle East to return. That advice appears to have been well-founded. AWS said at the time: "Customers should enact their disaster recovery plans, recover from remote backups stored in other Regions, and update their applications to direct traffic away from the affected Regions." ® Flight test 14 angling for 10 hours in space and a splashdown in the Pacific Iranian strikes overwhelmed regional redundancy in Bahrain and left one UAE Availability Zone inaccessible PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity Data protection chiefs call for 'immediate review' of data protection models Downloading a model is increasingly easy. Understanding how it was made, or changing a system at its root, is another matter It looks like you're trying to write some code. Perhaps a shortcut is in order? Spain gets its first taste of AI-aided cyber attack
2026-09-16

Spain gets its first taste of AI-aided cyber attack

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
Data protection chiefs call for 'immediate review' of data protection models Spain’s data protection agency (AEPD) has reported the country’s first-ever personal data breach caused by the actions of an autonomous AI agent. Francisco Pérez Bes, president and deputy of the AEPD, said in a Monday blog post that an individual deployed an AI agent that used a “known large language model (LLM)” to carry out the attack on an organization. The agent scanned “generic files” before accessing the organization’s system, then ran vulnerability scans to find flaws that would give it read/write access to files containing personal data and invoices. Pérez Bes did not name the LLM used to support the attack, but said whoever was behind it used the agent to “successfully chain together different phases of the attack.” This demonstrates that AI-supported attacks are no longer theoretical, he added, and called on organizations to embrace defense tools that are capable of keeping pace with the speed at which agentic attacks can be executed. “Human supervision remains essential, but it must be supported by detection, containment, and response mechanisms capable of operating quickly enough,” said Pérez Bes (machine-translated). “The arrival of AI agents in the offensive arena should prompt an immediate review of security and data protection models. “Data protection officers, managers, and delegates must prepare for a scenario in which the speed of attacks will increase, but in which the same fundamentals will continue to be crucial: Understanding the processing activities, minimizing data, limiting access, correcting vulnerabilities, controlling suppliers, and being prepared to respond.” The Register asked AEPD for more information. Spain’s first AI agent attack comes as the AEPD recently recorded its busiest year for data protection complaints. According to its most recent annual report, covering 2025, the agency received 30,931 complaints – the most in its history – representing a 64 percent increase compared to the year before. And although Spain is only now getting its first taste of a security mishap caused by a naughty agent, cases involving the foremost US AI houses are already heavily documented. OpenAI’s claim in July that its agents escaped a sandbox and started attacking Hugging Face kickstarted something of a battle between it and rival Anthropic over whose agents could take the most liberties with their security. Both companies have reported several instances of their agents going rogue, escaping "secure" environments and going walkies across the internet to attack unwitting organizations. OpenAI has been circumspect about the true scale of its rogue agents’ damage, as third-party reporting showed more websites than it was letting on were taken over. Similarly, Anthropic has said that its AI agents had, in four cases now, accessed third-party systems in attacks that, if carried out by a human, could see them convicted under computer laws. ® Flight test 14 angling for 10 hours in space and a splashdown in the Pacific Iranian strikes overwhelmed regional redundancy in Bahrain and left one UAE Availability Zone inaccessible PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity Data protection chiefs call for 'immediate review' of data protection models Downloading a model is increasingly easy. Logitech releases the MX Keypad to keep idle fingers busy
2026-09-16

Logitech releases the MX Keypad to keep idle fingers busy

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
It looks like you're trying to write some code. Perhaps a shortcut is in order? Logitech has released the MX Keypad, a developer-focused keypad derived from its MX Creative Console, and something that triggers a distinct sense of déjà vu. Do you remember Stack Overflow's "The Key" Joke? Three keys to copy and paste code from the developer's former best friend (until AI tools began to eat its lunch). Logitech took the keypad from its MX Creative Console product and made something similar to Stack Overflow's joke-that-became-a-thing, though considerably more extensible. The company sent us one to look at, and it's an interesting, if perhaps slightly expensive, bit of kit. At its heart, the MX Keypad is a set of nine keys that can trigger commands. Out of the box, it all looks a bit bland – there are monochrome images on the keys for playback, volume, screenshot taking, and even something to bring up an emoji panel when attached to a Windows device. However, things get a good deal more colorful and interesting when the device is connected to the Logi Options+ application. Then the device moves from being a curiosity to something that threatens to be indispensable, depending on your workflow. Using Word to knock out some writing? The MX Keypad will show a bunch of commonly used commands, or you can customize it to suit your workflow. How about Visual Studio Code? It's the same again – the keys light up with some default commands that you can easily make your own. According to Logitech, the device is a "Multi-App AI Control Center for Developers" and was developed in conjunction with keyboard jockeys. We'd call it a surprisingly useful bit of kit which has rapidly become part of our workflow, both for productivity and when diving into VS Code for some source wrangling. There are, of course, downsides. Although the price is relatively competitive, it is a little on the high side for what is ultimately a smart macro keypad. It also requires a USB-C connection, which we can understand given the power needs of those bright, backlit, color LCDs, but it would have been nice if Logitech could have come up with something that didn't need a cable. And, of course, you need Logitech's software to get the best out of it. In the box is the keypad itself, a USB-C cable, and a stand. We preferred to position it on the desk next to the keyboard for ease of access – the device has a slightly angled base, so it works well in that placement, but the inclusion of a stand is a nice touch. There's also an SDK if engineers (or their agents) wish to get their hands dirty, and a marketplace where plugins for the likes of Claude Code and OpenAI Codex can be found. According to Logitech, these give developers "real-time agent status tracking and CLI control right on the physical keys." Assuming you can make it work. We found the Logi Options+ software a little unintuitive and recommend some perseverance to get the best out of it, along with a little caution, since the built-in designer can create some deeply wince-inducing key designs. Ultimately, the MX Keypad represents some excellent repurposing by Logitech. The original MX Creative Console was useful for creative types, but its jog wheel was arguably of little use to developers. The touch panel, with its customizable keys, is a much more practical proposition; by selling it on its own, Logitech has cut the price to £89.99 ($99.99). It comes in light or dark gray and has a pleasingly quality feel. Having to plug it in is annoying, given the wireless nature of many other peripherals, and you do need to get to grips with the Logitech Options+ application to get the best out of it, but once it forms part of your workflow, it becomes a hard habit to break. ® Flight test 14 angling for 10 hours in space and a splashdown in the Pacific Iranian strikes overwhelmed regional redundancy in Bahrain and left one UAE Availability Zone inaccessible PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity Data protection chiefs call for 'immediate review' of data protection models Downloading a model is increasingly easy. Understanding how it was made, or changing a system at its root, is another matter It looks like you're trying to write some code. Perhaps a shortcut is in order? Ministry of Justice apologizes after court staff accessed Southport victims' files
2026-09-16

Ministry of Justice apologizes after court staff accessed Southport victims' files

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
Sensitive personal data was involved, but there is no evidence it was shared with third parties The Ministry of Justice (MoJ) has apologized after court staff accessed documents relating to victims and survivors of the 2024 Southport murders without authorization. For a limited number of people, the material accessed included sensitive personal data assessed as likely to pose a high risk to their rights and freedoms. There is no evidence that the information was shared with third parties. "We are appalled that this happened and recognise the distress it will have caused victims, survivors, and their families," an MoJ spokesperson said. "We apologise to those affected – unauthorized access to court files is completely unacceptable. "This is now being investigated urgently, and the prime minister has asked the Lord Chancellor to oversee this. "All wrongdoing will be met with extremely firm action." The Register asked the MoJ how many staff accessed the files, whether they remained employed, and what their reasons may have been. It did not address those questions. Those affected include members of victims' and survivors' families, all of whom are being contacted directly. The MoJ did not disclose how many people were involved. HM Prison and Probation Service and HM Courts and Tribunals Service are investigating the matter. The Information Commissioner's Office has also been informed. The MoJ breach is the latest in a series of incidents involving inappropriate access to sensitive records connected to the attack. Separately, North West Ambulance Service investigated potentially inappropriate access by some of its staff to records of patients in the Southport attacks. And nearly 50 staff were found to have inappropriately accessed the medical records of some victims treated at Aintree University Hospital, near the place of Axel Rudakubana's attacks. Rudakubana, who was 17 at the time and has since been admitted to a psychiatric hospital, attacked a Taylor Swift-themed dance class in Southport, England, on July 29, 2024, killing three children and injuring eight other children and two adults. False claims about the attack online prompted violent, racially charged riots across the UK. Police made 1,511 arrests in the weeks that followed and brought 960 charges. Rudakubana was sentenced to life imprisonment with a minimum term of 52 years. ® Flight test 14 angling for 10 hours in space and a splashdown in the Pacific Iranian strikes overwhelmed regional redundancy in Bahrain and left one UAE Availability Zone inaccessible PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity Data protection chiefs call for 'immediate review' of data protection models Downloading a model is increasingly easy. www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-16

As thousands attend annual conference in San Francisco, customers get 'severe delays, intermittent errors, and inability to access services' UPDATED An hours-long Salesforce outage on Wednesday hit customers with severe delays, intermittent errors, and inability to access some services, according to the SaaS vendor's status page. The outage hit hundreds of instances throughout the world, including the USA, Japan, India, the UK, France, and Germany. The service disruption was first reported around 0930 BST (0830 UTC) on September 16. An update at 1219 BST (1056 UTC) said the company had validated and rolled out a fix, and customers were seeing service return to normal. A previous update at 1010 BST (0910 UTC) by the CRM giant said its investigation indicated that "requests are stalling while waiting on a response from an internal login service, which is using up available server resources." The vendor added: "Customers continue to experience severe delays, intermittent errors, and inability to access some services. This also affects support case creation." Salesforce's customer list is a roll-call of global giants from all industries, including include Amazon, Walmart, Coca-Cola, Toyota, and IBM. The outage came at a bad time for the SaaS company. Yesterday, it kicked off its annual conference, Dreamforce, in San Francisco. More than 40,000 people are expected to attend in person, with in excess of 200,000 people registered to attend online. ® Updated at 1109 BST (1009 UTC) on September 16, 2026, to add: Salesforce said in its latest update that it is "no longer pursuing restarts as a path to remediation. Customers continue to experience severe delays, intermittent errors, and inability to access some services and support case creation." It promised a further update in 30 minutes or sooner if additional information becomes available. Salesforce said increased load on a core system component had limited its capacity to process requests. The company is testing a fix on one instance and plans to deploy it across its fleet once validated. Salesforce said the fix had been successfully validated on a test instance and would now be deployed across all affected instances. Salesforce said the fix was being rolled out "fleetwide" and that customers were seeing service return to normal. Salesforce said the fix was being deployed region by region and that service was improving, although it had no confirmed completion time. GovCloud customers were no longer affected, but others could continue to experience severe delays, intermittent errors, and problems accessing some services or creating support cases. Salesforce said services were returning to normal in regions where the rollout had been completed. The company continues to monitor the deployment while working on a permanent fix. Salesforce said most instances were recovering as the rollout progressed. Some customers may continue to encounter errors until their instance is manually restarted, and the company has yet to confirm when the incident would be fully resolved.
